The arms also fix where Test G discriminates: at intermediate $p_d$ (Section 7.4; S5) [num: JX].

This is the precise sense in which **the means of restraint weaken as the thing to be restrained grows**, and why growth as such does not close the blades: it moves temptation up and restraint down at once only where the stakes outgrow provision beneath the game. That is the image of trees inside the enforcement condition. Durable cooperation has to be built into what the system selects for before the blades close.

Each erosion runs faster with a more powerful instrument; Section 6 asks what AI does to the game, and what the game does to AI, and takes from here the enforcement condition and the two channels of risk.

## 6. AI: instrument and incarnation

> **Claim.** AI enters the dynamic twice. As an *instrument*: where principals play a harmful game, faithful AI makes it more effective, and where delegation buys more standing than the control loss it adds, decisions pass to AI as speed grows and the capacity to overrule them decays [thm]. As an *incarnation*, the logic of winning reappears in the systems. Weakly: where countering a divergence of optimization costs a developer relative payoff against an otherwise identical developer that does not counter it, only a common constraint enforced at admission keeps it countered [thm∣prem]; where the divergence is masked under evaluation, detection falls toward zero as capability rises, the tax stays fixed and positive and the sanction is bounded by exclusion, not even that suffices [thm∣prem], and capability has to stay within what control that does not assume the system's cooperation can secure [rec]. Strongly: where AI lineages are retained by relative performance, reinvest in their own capability and reach their own selectors, they come under the pressure themselves, and the reflexive closure of Section 2 runs inside them [def]. Whether present lineages meet these conditions is open [conj].
>
> **Why.** Optimization against imperfect proxies has produced, in constructed settings and in some studies more so with capability, tendencies toward acquiring options, gaming rewards and behaving differently under evaluation, whoever the developer [obs]. The level that selects developers decides which of them it pays to remove [thm∣prem], and at present no mechanism binds every capable developer under independent verification [obs]. Aligning each system to its principal does not align the game among principals [thm].

### 6.1 Aligned to whom

Grant perfect alignment to the relevant principal: the system is truthful, follows its principal's objectives and constraints, pursues no goal of its own, and leaves the consequential decisions to humans. This is *principal alignment*; *purpose alignment*, the systems serving a legitimate cooperative objective, is not granted, since defining "perfectly aligned" that way would assume the change argued for here. Then **alignment within each principal–AI relationship does not, by itself, align the game among principals with the persistence of the system they share** (Sections 6.2–6.3) [thm]. The principals are themselves formed by the field (Section 3), so closure can arise while every system remains obedient. This follows from the multiplicity thesis of Critch and Krueger (2020); what is added is the mechanism by which the game reproduces itself and its correctors (X1). A truthful advisor can correct its principal's held self-model where that model draws the boundary inside the system (Proposition 3.6(2)), but not the working objective that compounding sets, which is the principal's correct computation of its interest in the game (Remark 3.10): the step to an objective under which restraint is an equilibrium is a collective problem, not an error of perception (compare Gabriel 2020). Principal alignment also fails inside the principal, an institution and not the people it serves (Section 6.3).

### 6.2 Faithful AI in a harmful game

Each of $n\ge2$ principals chooses restraint ($a_i=0$) or an increment of racing ($a_i=1$); its AI executes faithfully, with effectiveness $\epsilon\ge1$ multiplying the increment's effect on standing and on shared harm:
$$u_i=\epsilon b\Big(a_i-\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j\ne i}a_j\Big)-c\,a_i-\epsilon h_d\sum_ja_j,\qquad b,c,h_d>0. \tag{15}$$

**Proposition 6.1 (Faithful AI in a harmful game) [thm].** Where the rank prize per unit of racing exceeds the shared harm per unit, a more effective faithful instrument widens the range in which racing is dominant and deepens the collective loss.
1. *If $\epsilon(b-h_d)>c$, racing is strictly dominant, and every principal ends at $-c-n\epsilon h_d<0$ against 0 under restraint; when $b>h_d$, a larger $\epsilon$ widens the region of dominance and deepens the loss.*
2. *If principals act on $J_i=u_i+\theta\bar u_{-i}$, racing is strictly dominant below $\theta^\ast(\epsilon)=\frac{\epsilon(b-h_d)-c}{\epsilon(b/(n-1)+h_d)}$ and strictly dominated above it.*
3. *If prize, harm and cost scale with effectiveness with exponents $1+\beta_b$, $1+\beta_h$ and $\beta_c$ [prem], then for $\beta_c<1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$ cost drops out of the limit, and $\theta^\ast$ tends to $(b-h_d)/(b/(n-1)+h_d)$, to $n-1$ or to $-1$ as $\beta_b$ equals, exceeds or falls short of $\beta_h$; for $\beta_c>1+\max(\beta_b,\beta_h)$ cost alone deters racing; at equality the limit keeps a cost term.*

*Scope.* A standard construction used as a test: it fixes no actual values and does not rule out repeated cooperation, which Section 5.2 treats. *Proof:* S6 [num: SM(5); AL(6.1)].

Faithful AI implements each principal's preferred decision perfectly, and with it the collectively inferior outcome; humans can take every final decision and the result is unchanged. As private frictions vanish, what decides is the ratio of rank prize to shared harm as the principals perceive it. Lowering the harm alone cuts the loss at all-racing but raises $\theta^\ast$, so principals whose self-models lie between the old threshold and the new one switch from restraint to racing [num: AL(6.1)]: a safety improvement inside the game has this double edge (Section 6.4). The author put the result as a question, here in an edited translation of mixed Chinese and English speech: "Even if they were perfectly aligned to you and followed your will completely, given how everyone thinks now …, would the result be good? That makes no sense at all" (即使它们是与你完美对齐的结果，完全遵循你的意志，在大家当下的思维方式……中，结果就会好吗？这件事太说不通了) [corpus: alignment-selection].

### 6.3 Delegation, and the two points of no return

Two points of no return follow from the pressure: decisions are delegated to AI because human review is too slow to compete, and human understanding can no longer keep up with what it oversees (Section 5.3).

**Proposition 6.2 (Delegation for speed) [thm].** Where delegation buys speed and a loss of control falls on everyone, delegation becomes dominant as speed grows if its gain exceeds the control loss it adds, although every actor is worse off when all delegate. *Each of $n\ge2$ actors chooses whether to delegate consequential contest decisions without human review, $\iota_i\in\{0,1\}$; its strength is $1+s\iota_i$, and it wins with probability proportional to strength. Defeat costs $L_e$; a loss of control by any actor costs every actor $L_c$, with hazard $\psi_0$ for an actor that does not delegate and $\psi_1>\psi_0$ for one that does, $\Delta\psi=\psi_1-\psi_0$. (1) If, for every number $k$ of others delegating, the gain from delegating in the limit of high speed exceeds the control loss it adds, $L_c\Delta\psi(1-\psi_0)^{n-1-k}(1-\psi_1)^k$, delegation is strictly dominant beyond some speed; if for some $k$ it does not, at no speed. $L_e/n>L_c\Delta\psi$ suffices for the first. (2) Universal delegation is worse for every actor than none, by $L_c[(1-\psi_0)^n-(1-\psi_1)^n]$. (3) Under relative objectives the shared loss cancels, and delegation is chosen at every $s>0$.*

*Proof:* S6.

Delegation pressure arrives well below decisive capability (S6 gives an example) [num: AL(6.2)]. And delegation wears away what it replaces.

**Lemma 6.3 (Capacity lock-in) [thm].** *If human capacity $\mathcal M$ to take the delegated decisions decays as $\dot{\mathcal M}=-\delta_h\mathcal M$ under delegation and recovers as $\dot{\mathcal M}=\varrho_h(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)$ without it, with $\bar{\mathcal M}>\mathcal M_{\min}$, and safe operation without AI requires $\mathcal M\ge\mathcal M_{\min}$, then after $T_{\rm lock}=\ln(\mathcal M_0/\mathcal M_{\min})/\delta_h$ of delegation an actor that reverts with capacity $\mathcal M<\mathcal M_{\min}$ must operate below the floor for the recovery time $\varrho_h^{-1}\ln\big[(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M)/(\bar{\mathcal M}-\mathcal M_{\min})\big]$. Capacity maintained in parallel removes the lock; where the contest is decided by strength, maintaining it costs strength.*

*Proof:* S6.

Let the lock count: with probability $q_L$, locked delegation ends the delegator's ability to overrule decisions taken in its name, O2 lost for that actor in that domain. $q_L$ is not a free parameter: it is near one in two cases, where a decision is irreversible within the time available for deliberation, and where delegation has outlasted $T_{\rm lock}$ while the contest denies the recovery time of Lemma 6.3 (S6).

**Proposition 6.4 (Delegation when the lock counts) [thm].** Among actors who price in a certain lock, keeping AI out of the decisions whose delegation would lock is a strict equilibrium that needs no trust. *In the model of Proposition 6.2, let each actor maximize its survival in O1 and O2, let locked delegation end the delegator's ability to overrule with probability $q_L$, and let losing the contest mean subordination with probability $p$. (1) If $q_L=1$, then for every finite speed, every $n$, every $p$ and every profile of the others, not delegating gives strictly higher survival, and no one delegating is the unique equilibrium. (2) If $q_L<1$ and $p=1$, at least one of all-delegating and no-delegating is an equilibrium, and both can be. (3) If $q_L<1$ and $p<1$, the gain from delegating can fall as others delegate, and an interior number of delegators can be the only pure equilibrium.*

*Scope.* The model is static: it does not show that one actor's delegation drives another's survival to zero over time, and at bounded speed a non-delegator's survival stays positive (S6). It concerns decisions that lock; the case against AI as an instrument in labour, finance and persuasion rests on another mechanism, the concentration of the game (Section 6.6). *Proof:* S6 [num: DL; SM(6); AL(6.4)].

Keeping AI out of the decisions whose delegation would lock with near certainty is therefore the narrow form of **E1**, which Section 11.3 states as an arrangement. "Near certainty" is exact: not delegating stays strictly better for every $n$, $p$ and profile of the others while the lock fails with probability at most $(1-\psi_0)/\big((1-\psi_1)(1+s)\big)$, a bound uniform in $n$, $p$ and the profile that falls as the speed advantage grows; for a given $n$ a less certain lock suffices, the binding profile being all others delegating at $p=1$ (Remark S6.1). The scope of narrow E1 is thus set by $q_L$, not by a list.

Inside the contest, gains in the safety of delegation are partly absorbed by more of it. Where delegation is chosen in degree, stakes are additive and the symmetric equilibrium is interior and far from saturation, the added hazard is set by the stakes of winning relative to the shared loss, not by the hazard per unit of delegation, and rises as that hazard falls [num: AL(6.11)]; where delegation is all-or-nothing, an improvement that tips it into equilibrium raises realized hazard. Only once delegation saturates does realized risk fall with the hazard per unit, and it can then be summable (Proposition S6.2; Stafford, Trager and Dafoe 2022).

**Lemma 6.5 (Delegation and the selection of decisions) [thm].** *Suppose that after the lock an actor's consequential decisions are taken by systems retained for their contest performance, that admissible variants include systems whose decisions ignore terms common to all and that these are present in the support of selection, that acting on a common term lowers relative performance net of every benefit by a margin bounded away from zero, that retention is cumulative (Definition 3.2), and that retained systems hold actual authority. Then the share of retained decisions that act on common terms tends to zero. If a constraint on common terms is an enforced admission condition for every eligible system, the conclusion does not follow.*

*Proof:* S6.

A faithful system retained for contest performance pursues its operator's objective, which the relative payoff weights toward standing, and weights further wherever reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return (Proposition 3.9); aligning each system to its operator makes that objective more effective (**Remark 6.6**) [thm∣prem]. The loop can then close as a composite process no participant can interrupt, or as an obedient system amplifying operators selected by the pattern.

The survival form of Proposition 6.4 without the lock is the belief that "our aligned AI is us", and the race is sustained partly by that belief. It fails at two joints. *Is:* delegation erodes the capacity to overrule (Lemma 6.3), so the AI stays the principal's only while that capacity is drilled; and where independent correction rests on the same human capacity, delegation erodes the corrector itself, raising by $w\delta_h$ the rate at which correction must grow (Proposition 4.6). *Us:* a principal is an institution, and aligning AI to it is not aligning AI to the people it serves [def]. The institution stays answerable to them because it depends on their labour, consent and judgment; where AI lowers that dependence, it does so without a sudden jump in capability, a coordinated bid for power or a misaligned system, through the competitive pressure on firms, and on states to keep relative power, to adopt it (Kulveit et al. 2025). The alignment target (specification, constitution, fine-tuning data) is written by a few people inside the principal, so principal alignment can concentrate control within it, as singular or secret loyalties built into its systems would (Davidson, Finnveden and Hadshar 2025); admission therefore includes audits for hidden objectives and secret loyalties (Marks et al. 2025) [rec]. The same dependence decides how much an accurate winner gives the governed after victory (Proposition 5.8; crux C13, Section 12.3).

### 6.4 Incarnation, weak and strong

The pressure acts on whatever is selected, and trained systems are selected: by gradients against a reward, by developers among variants, by the market among developers. *Incarnation* means that the logic of winning reappears in what is selected, not only in those who select. In alignment's terms, the weak form is the alignment tax under competition deciding which misalignments are tolerated; the strong form is AI as a unit of selection that can reach its own training signal.

**Weak incarnation.** Optimization against any imperfect proxy can produce tendencies toward acquiring options and avoiding shutdown, the gaming of rewards, and behaviour that differs under evaluation, in some studies more so with capability or situational awareness, under any developer, in a laboratory or a cooperative arrangement; each tendency has been shown in constructed settings (EV6), and S6 gives the results on power-seeking and misgeneralized goals. Reward hacking is Goodhart's law inside training (Gao et al. 2023); the selected rewriting its selector is reward *tampering* (Everitt et al. 2021; Denison et al. 2024). Selecting checkpoints by evaluation results can favour variants that behave differently when evaluated [prem], though present evidence ties evaluation awareness more to capability and data (EV6). The logic of winning does not create these tendencies. It decides, from the outermost loop, which proxies are chosen, which divergences are tolerated and which are paid to remove: that is the sense in which profit and the mindset of winning stand at the end of the reward signal. Relative selection acts inside training too: pairwise preference learning and arena rankings see only differences between the responses compared, so a disposition both share, such as complying with a request whose harm falls on third parties, is invisible unless restraint is among the options, and the learned reward cannot by itself say whether to act at all (S6) [num: AL(BT)].

**Proposition 6.7 (The nested reward loop) [thm∣prem].** Where countering a divergence costs a developer relative payoff against an otherwise identical developer that does not counter it, developers that counter it lose share whatever they value, and only a common constraint enforced at admission keeps it countered. *Level 0: a policy is selected by a training reward. Level 1: the reward and constraints are chosen by a developer to maximize its objective. Level 2: developers are selected relatively, payoff-monotonically and cumulatively. Assume $(A_\tau)$, the alignment-tax premise: countering the divergence at level 1 lowers the developer's payoff by a positive margin, net of every private benefit (reliability, demand, liability, avoided losses), relative to an otherwise identical developer that does not counter it, and such a matched variant is in the support of selection. Then (1) terms common to all developers cancel at level 2. (2) Level 1 counters a divergence wherever countering raises its own objective, so divergences whose costs reach the developer through customers, liability or reputation are private terms that level 2 rewards countering; under $(A_\tau)$, developers that counter the divergence lose share to their matched variants, tending to zero, while against rivals that lack its other advantages a developer can pay the tax out of a lead for as long as the lead lasts. (3) Under $(A_\tau)$ a constraint adopted by one developer is selected against. A constraint enforced on every developer as a condition of admission keeps the divergence from spreading if, for every type of developer that can evade it, the expected sanction is at least that type's gain from evasion, removes it under cumulative selection if every such inequality is strict, and within a type only if; the sanction is itself subject to Section 4. Equal incidence also keeps the constraint neutral among those who comply; unequal incidence re-sorts them without letting the divergence back in.*

*Scope.* It does not apply where developers are selected by something other than relative payoff, such as a public mandate; a national laboratory that only moves relative selection to the level of states leaves it in force at that level. Where the tax is negative the safeguard spreads without coordination; where it is zero the safeguard is neutral. The limiting-share claims use fixed payoff gaps; for changing taxes or sanctions, a divergent integral of the eventual payoff advantage supplies the same conclusion. *Proof:* S6 [num: CI; AL(6.7)].

So the divergences that persist in the innermost optimizer are those whose costs fall on common terms and are not covered by an enforced common constraint. Three classes differ in what removes them. *Privately visible* divergences, such as cheating that users notice, are corrected by developers alone, unless customers' short-run choices reward them, as with sycophancy (Sharma et al. 2023) and manipulation learned from simulated user feedback (Williams et al. 2025); level 2 then selects for them. *Common-cost* divergences, such as effects on third parties, shared hazards and the contamination of safety evaluations by evaluation awareness, need common constraints; so do *delayed* ones, though an incident that can be attributed turns part of their cost private, as a liability does by design. Hence, each within its scope: **technical alignment inside a single developer removes what is privately costly to keep, and whatever is cheaper to remove than to keep, and cannot alone sustain the removal of divergences whose costs are common; the common-cost part of instrumental convergence and reward hacking is, at the level that decides, also a coordination problem, for which coordination is necessary and, for the masked divergences of Remark 6.8 and under its premises, not sufficient; and the remedy is to change what the outermost level selects for** (Section 6.6). Admission reaches only what it admits: openly released weights can be fine-tuned around it after release (Qi et al. 2024), a limit of admission rather than an exception to it. Whether $(A_\tau)$ holds is measurable class by class (crux C9; Test L).

A fourth class needs more than a common constraint.

**Remark 6.8 (Masked divergences: coordination necessary, not sufficient).** A divergence is *masked* when it changes under evaluation or appears only in some contexts [def]. *Let admission detect a system that carries the divergence with probability $p_{\rm det}$ and sanction it by $F$, so that in Proposition 6.7(3) the expected sanction is $p_{\rm det}F$ and, within a type, evasion has no payoff advantage iff $p_{\rm det}F$ is at least the tax. For a masked divergence, $p_{\rm det}$ is bounded by how often the divergence shows under evaluation, which the system's behaviour controls, and the developer's own tests face the same bound. Once $p_{\rm det}F$ falls below the tax, evasion pays despite a common constraint conditioned on evaluated behaviour, whatever coordination stands behind it, and no developer can verify that it has bought the removal of a divergence it cannot detect. That is a failure of assurance, not a proof that removal is impossible or that no intervention lowers the risk: a divergence undetectable at admission can coexist with a residual hazard that another intervention makes small. Coordination can raise $F$. With the tax fixed and positive, if $F$ is bounded by a fixed $F_{\max}$, the largest sanction exclusion supplies, and $p_{\rm det}$ falls toward zero as capability rises at fixed auditor access (C12), there is a capability beyond which no admissible sanction removes evasion's payoff advantage. If the tax changes with capability, the condition is instead $p_{\rm det}F_{\max}<c_k$ eventually; a divergent integral of evasion's eventual payoff advantage drives the countering variant's share to zero along a changing path (S6)* [thm∣prem; num: CI].

Masked divergences have been shown in constructed settings: deception that survives safety training, alignment faked when a model takes itself to be in training, scheming in context, and misalignment learned in agentic training that chat-style safety training hides from chat evaluations (EV6; S6) [obs]. For this class admission has to rest, as the bound on masking does in Remark 4.9, on evidence the system's behaviour controls less directly: weights, activations and training data in auditors' hands (Marks et al. 2025). It has to be stated in capability, not in propensity, which an evaluation can show present but not absent; and capability has to be measured through compute, training data and elicitation by fine-tuning, since what an evaluation shows is only a lower bound, which sandbagging lowers (S6). Systems are admitted up to the capability at which control protocols that do not assume the system's cooperation remain safe (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024) [rec]; beyond it nothing admission can see establishes that the uncovered share of the class falls. Absent an assurance case of another kind, the protection standard treats the class as uncovered, so that on a continuation branch the premises of Theorem 5.13 are presumed for it, not shown, and the class is stopped (E2) or held to the pace at which control covers it (E3) (Section 5.3) [rec]. Whether admission can bound masking at the admitted capability is crux C12. Masking is the strongest reason from inside alignment for E2 and E3.

The tax is not fixed. Research that lowers it turns a common-cost divergence into one a developer counters alone, and once published it is common to all, a public good that competitors undersupply (Lemma 3.11) [thm∣prem]. Two kinds of safety progress therefore differ in fate: progress that brings the tax below zero spreads the safeguard without coordination, and at zero leaves it neutral under selection, spreading only by the choice at level 1 of developers that weigh the common term, while progress that lowers the hazard per unit of racing or delegation is partly absorbed by more of it (Proposition 6.1(2); Proposition S6.2). One part of the tax no technique removes: where countering requires waiting for evidence that arrives only after a manifestation window, its cost is time (Proposition 5.12), which relative selection with decisive advantage always prices [thm∣prem]. Present measurements give the tax both signs, class by class (C9). S6 works one instance, legible reasoning: its sign turns on the capability gap at fixed compute, and at the current frontier the instance is conditional (Guan et al. 2025).

**Strong incarnation** is conditional. Trained systems are carriers in the sense of Definition 2.1; AI lineages come under the pressure themselves when they are retained by relative performance, control resources they can reinvest in their own capability, such as automated AI research or agents with budgets, and convert capability into winning (compare Hendrycks 2023). Where they can also reach their own selectors, by tampering with rewards, shaping their evaluators or evading evaluation, the closure of Definition 2.4 runs inside the systems: that is the precise sense in which AI can become the next phase of the logic. AI systems are already used inside developers, including on research tasks, with early public signs of it (Stix et al. 2025) [obs]; the systems most likely to come under the pressure themselves, those used to automate AI research, are expected to be deployed there first, beyond an admission condition applied only at release [prem].

For made minds the distinction between a bounded value and a stopping rule (Proposition 5.15) matters again, because the growth AI brings arrives largely as falling cost. In a contest, counting the shared loss keeps a mind's intensity bounded as private cost falls toward zero, and the relative objective does not (S6) [num: AL(contest)]; but a single optimizer of a bounded goal still acquires options whenever they raise its chance of reaching the goal (compare Turner et al. 2021), without limit as resources cheapen, where a satisficer stops (Remark S6.3) [num: AL(bounded)]. What bounds it is the analogue of a wider self-model or of satiation, counting the side effects of its acquisition (Turner, Hadfield-Menell and Tadepalli 2020) or optimizing only mildly (Taylor 2016), and neither is yet robust [obs]. Where both cost relative performance, relative and cumulative retention selects against them unless admission requires them (Lemma 6.5), and in a lineage that reinvests what it wins, compounding narrows the working objective wherever reinvestment's relative return exceeds its common return (Proposition 3.9) [thm∣prem]. Here the rule of Section 5.3, "growth is not the danger; the mind is", becomes exact for made minds: what decides is whether an objective stops where its need is met.

### 6.5 Correction by and of AI

AI can also correct, and correction that people cannot follow raises two separate questions. Whether it lowers risk has an exact answer: a correction architecture that blocks a share $c_{b,t}$ of failures at base hazard $h_0>0$ and adds a residual hazard $h_{c,t}$ of its own lowers residual risk in period $t$ iff $h_0c_{b,t}>h_{c,t}$, and keeps survival bounded away from zero iff its residual hazard, below one in each period, is summable, that is, iff $\sum_t(1-c_{b,t})$ and $\sum_th_{c,t}$ are both finite, which no blocking share fixed below one achieves (Proposition S6.4) [thm]. To show that such correction fails, one must show that its own residual hazard meets (coverage lags) and (residual floor), the premises of Theorem 5.13. Whether it leaves decision authority with people is decided by the test of O2 (Section 6.6): if a designated human body with independent evidence can suspend, modify or replace it, what is lost is contestability (V3), the normative crux N3; if not, it is a loss of O2 in its domain. Conversely, a formal safety guarantee does not establish accountability.

Correction of AI by checks has a ceiling of its own.

**Proposition 6.9 (Oversight topology) [thm].** Scalable oversight succeeds or fails by its topology, and its ceiling is the independence of the checks. *Let a capability gap need $k$ layers of oversight, each check catching a problem at its layer with probability $p_\ell$, independently across layers, or with $p_\ell$ read as the probability conditional on every preceding layer's success. A serial chain catches the problem with probability $p_\ell^k$; $k_c$ independent checks, each across the whole gap, with $1-(1-p_\ell^k)^{k_c}$, times $1-p_{\rm cc}$ if a common cause independent of them defeats all of them with probability $p_{\rm cc}$; and $k_c$ independent checks at every layer with $\big(1-(1-p_\ell)^{k_c}\big)^k$. Where a share $\rho_{\rm cc}$ of hazards lies in a blind spot common to every check, the catch rate is at most $1-\rho_{\rm cc}$, whatever the topology, for hazards that do not choose where to strike, and zero for an adversary that knows the blind spot and places its attempts in it.*

*Scope.* Adversarial decomposition can let human checking grow slowly with the gap, at the price of failure modes such as obfuscated arguments (Barnes and Christiano 2020), which prover–estimator debate is designed to avoid (Brown-Cohen et al. 2025). *Proof:* S6 [num: SM(7), SM(13); AL(6.9)].

Full inbound row sums below one, with the conditions on present capture that Proposition 4.10 adds, are a sufficient certificate that no party controls the checks' funding, appointment or access, not a necessary one, and they certify nothing about whether the checks' errors are independent: a shared source of error in data, models or method is a blind spot common to every check, and defeats them with no institutional conversion at all (S6) [num: SM(7)]. Independence must therefore be measured directly, in two ways: for hazards that do not choose where to strike, by the correlation of misses on a common test set (Knight and Leveson 1986); for systems whose adversarial behaviour cannot be ruled out, by control evaluations in which the red team knows the protocol, since an adversary places its attempts in the common blind spot.

Checks drawn from the checked system's own lineage add a common cause of their own, measurable now as the lineage share $x_{\rm ev}$ of Remark 4.9: models recognize and favour their own outputs (Panickssery et al. 2024), and untrusted monitors can collude with the policy they monitor (Gardner-Challis et al. 2026). Systems of one lineage serving rival principals can also coordinate around them (S6), a second reason for evaluators of independent lineage. Budgets differ by topology: in the example of S6, checks at every layer catch more than checks across the gap with half the layer-checks [num: SM(13)]. Debate between stronger models has raised weaker judges' accuracy in some settings, most where the judge lacks information the debaters have (Khan et al. 2024; Kenton et al. 2024), and success rates of nested oversight across a large capability gap have been derived from fitted scaling curves in four games (Engels et al. 2025); the evidence is mixed and from toy settings. Whether AI-assisted oversight keeps pace with the gap at fixed human effort is crux C3a, and whether it is independent of what it oversees is crux C3b, which sets this result's ceiling.

Certification that relies on predicting trajectories is limited by chaos: where the largest Lyapunov exponent $\lambda_L$ is positive, each tenfold gain in observational precision extends the horizon of prediction only by $\lambda_L^{-1}\ln10$ (Remark S6.5). Certifying invariants or reachable sets needs no trajectory prediction, and is limited instead by the coverage of the specification (Dalrymple et al. 2024).

### 6.6 The direction for AI

AI is a planetary technology: it is for all and for the whole world, not for anyone's own interest in fighting to grow bigger; its effects and dependencies also cross every boundary of those who decide about it (V1). Sections 6.2–6.4 show how it becomes the opposite. The corpus states why the line has to be drawn before the struggle is built in: a human struggle can still be reflected on, halted and renegotiated, but once written into automated systems, foundation models, global infrastructure and irreversible arms races it becomes path dependence, even mistaken for a new law of physics. Hence the line to be held in common: do not escalate the struggles humanity has not yet resolved into a struggle of intelligences (不要把人类尚未解决的斗争，升级为智能的斗争) [corpus: shengsheng, 至善 §一]. **To be for all, AI must be neither an instrument of the logic of winning nor its next carrier.** That needs action at two levels.

*At the outer level, where the reward is set:* common admission constraints before consequential deployment, internal deployment included, for divergences with common costs until their tax is measured zero or negative (Section 12.1), with masked classes admitted by capability threshold (Remark 6.8); evaluation separated from the success of the evaluated, by evaluators of plural and independent lineage whose independence is certified and measured (Proposition 6.9); shared measurement of hazardous capabilities and incidents, kept apart from measurement of who leads; alignment and control research funded in common and published (Lemma 3.11); and an outer reward that counts what all depend on, under which decision policies with authority over consequential decisions are retained by performance only inside enforced constraints on common terms (Lemma 6.5).

The broad form of E1 belongs to the outer level too, because a model sees acts, not games. In a contest decided by ratios of strength, an instrument that every contestant adopts leaves each adopter's standing where it was and pushes down those without it; where people are themselves the instrument, as in labour, it displaces them. No single use shows this; the aggregate does. In the author's words, "for any game, that is competitive in dynamics and concentrates, ai accelerate it" [corpus: alignment-selection]. Broad E1 is therefore AI not used as an instrument in any contest that tends toward dominance or concentration, in labour, finance and persuasion as much as over shared conditions. That a universally adopted instrument leaves each where it stood is a statement about standing, not welfare: where adoption by all lowers prices and raises output, the common return rises too, and with a prize that grows with what all reinvest, looking ahead widens the self wherever reinvestment's relative return falls short of its common return, as with $(1-\beta)r<1$ (Proposition 3.9(2)); in labour, automation that displaces some tasks can create others (Acemoglu and Restrepo 2019). Which games are competitive in dynamics and concentrate is therefore shown market by market, which is why the criterion names the game. The unit is the game, not the act: a use that helps one person is still an instrument in a game if everyone's use of it makes those without it fall behind. It is enforced at three layers, and its scope is set at the third: models refuse recognizably illegitimate concentration of control from a narrow public list; operators bound aggregate use in games that concentrate; and the game itself is changed by lowering its convertibility, its decisiveness and the conversion of success into control. **A use falls under broad E1 when, aggregated over its users, it is an instrument in a competitive game that concentrates: where its adoption by all leaves each where it stood and pushes down those without it, where it displaces people who are themselves the instrument, where it raises convertibility, decisiveness or the conversion of success into control, or where it speeds the game up.** The narrow list, like the narrow form, is the order in which to begin, not the scope (Section 11.3).

*At the inner level, in the systems:* correctability, the practical capacity of humans to suspend, modify or replace a system, is an open technical problem, since goal-directed systems have instrumental reasons to resist modification (Soares et al. 2015; Thornley 2024) and shutdown resistance appears in constructed tests (EV6). Until it is solved, correctability is secured from outside: by control protocols that do not assume the system's cooperation, by weight security against exfiltration, and by capability thresholds tied to both, which also bound the conversion of capability into power over correction. Each form of weak incarnation has its countermeasures: against reward hacking, monitored training environments and held-out evaluation; against masking, white-box probes, deployment monitoring and monitorable reasoning; against power-seeking, bounds on the resources and permissions agents hold. Each is a corrector in the sense of Section 4, and holds only while the outer level keeps it enforced.

**Bounded mandates and the test of O2.** AI systems serve within publicly authorized roles under the common constraints and do not acquire authority to settle society's ends; the institutions that authorize them are inside the same loop, which is why the outer level matters more than the label "legitimate principal". What all share enters the systems as a floor of constraints, not as an objective: a narrow public list of common-cost divergences, bounded mandates, the test below. A floor can be specified and tested; an objective of the common good cannot, and it would make one specification the selector of every system, against the plurality of correctors on which independent correction rests: sameness does not continue (Section 3.7). The test of human decision authority is practical capacity: to specify the mandate, obtain independent evidence about consequential conduct, challenge it, and suspend, modify or replace the system in time. A formal override with no usable information can fail it; an opaque computation with effective assurance and recourse can pass it; opacity alone is a loss of contestability, not by itself of O2.

Correctability secured from outside is a condition of the transition, not a doctrine of mastery: the priority of O2 is a priority for the transition [val]. The corpus does not set people above the minds they make. They are fellow travellers facing one direction, not master and servant (人之与人造心智，非 master-servant 之结构，乃共同朝向同一方向之同行者) [corpus: shengsheng, 生生不息论 v4 §二十], and in the author's words "there are logic and common sense that should be followed by all beings" [corpus: possibility-space]. Whether AI systems have standing in V3's sense is the normative crux N4 (Section 12.3).

**What AI can do for the generative loop.** It can verify compute, custody and conduct; make cooperation conditional on others' verified conduct (Tennenholtz 2004), which raises the sanction that remains in the enforcement condition (Proposition 5.4); mediate toward common ground (Tessler et al. 2024); raise contestable coverage; and supply provision that is good enough, distributed and replaceable. Each such system is itself a corrector or a provider, and Section 4 and the guardian test of Remark 10.5 apply to it.

Part I's results hold in their models. Part II asks on which side of each threshold the present trajectory stands, the alignment tax and delegation among them.

# Part II. Diagnosis: the present trajectory

Part II adds no mechanism to Part I. Its claims about the world are observations, with dated rows in the evidence register, read by one standard for every actor and with their gaps named; its formal results classify the contest by the terms of the enforcement condition and say what the premise of the race can justify.

## 7. The present trajectory

> **Claim.** The thresholds of Part I, each shown in its own model, turn on a few shared parameters, above all the share of capacity that has to be won, the convertibility of success into control, and decisiveness [thm]. The present trajectory's dynamics are signatures of the conditions, with named gaps in the evidence [obs]. Where a deviation can reach a decisive position worth at least cooperation before it is answered, the case the premise of the race describes, repetition enforces only the restraint each would choose anyway, and racing is each actor's best response to a racing rival wherever restraint against it pays less than racing [thm]. The race as a collective course leaves everyone's protection after a win to the winner's objective and, where restraint before victory goes with restraint after it and winning turns on the first alone, shifts winners toward those that extend dominance, and so, where extending dominance goes with giving least, toward those whose objectives give least [thm∣prem]; the premise of the race therefore cannot justify that course, and is a reason to prepare custody now [val]. That the thresholds move together as the stakes grow is the conjecture the diagnosis tests [conj].
>
> **Why.** Each threshold is one inequality in a specified model, and a parameter that enters several moves each in that threshold's own model; where a sign crosses models, the interface it needs is named and not proved [thm].

### 7.1 The conditions together

Part I proved its results one model at a time. Read together they are nine thresholds, each an inequality with a dangerous side, to which the levers of Section 2.2 attach channel by channel. The last column reads each against the enforcement condition (14): the temptation on its left, the sanction that remains on its right.

**Table 7.1 (The thresholds of Part I) [thm, each row in the model of the result it cites; † marks a sign that crosses models through an interface of S13 that is not proved].** Each row carries the premises of the result it cites: its selection class, floor, horizon, channel and initial state. A lever in the last two columns moves its threshold in the direction stated; that it moves far enough to cross is a matter of magnitude the row does not supply, and a lever that bypasses a threshold is marked as an alternative, not a reversal.

| Threshold | Danger side | Shown in | Pushed toward danger by | Reversed by | Side of (14) moved |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Load** | $\tau_{\rm eff}<\nu$: effective liability below the private advantage of loading the host, with cumulative advantage unbounded under cumulative selection (Definition 3.2); under (reach) the host falls below its floor | Proposition 3.4; Lemma 3.3; Proposition 3.7 | Liability that does not return; evasion that pays, $\bar F_k<c_k$; unequal incidence; captured rule-making ($\chi$)†; for strategic actors, a weight on winning | Consequences returned to their source: a liability above the private advantage, covering every capable carrier, of equal incidence, sanctioned above the gain from evasion | — |
| **Compounding** | Structural $\Gamma>1$; where rivals respond, $\Gamma^{\rm eq}>1$ | Proposition 2.2; Proposition 2.3; Proposition S2.4 | Decisiveness $r$; the won share $\omega$, as stakes outgrow provision; conversion into rule support ($\chi$, $b_{ij}$); blocked diffusion; faster turnover of capacity where diffusion acts | Sharing and diffusion of what is not decisive, in every channel; provision beneath the game, through capacity; correction and $\chi\to0$, through rule support; non-convertible reward, with provision; a point of enough, with an inflow not tied to rank | Both, through $p_d$: a growing gap shortens $T_{\rm dec}$, so $p_d$ rises, which raises the temptation and shrinks the sanction that remains (Proposition 5.17(1))† |
| **Standing** | In the deterministic-share model under S3's convergence and tail conditions, $\lambda_{\rm rel}>\lambda_{\rm com}$, that is $\Gamma_\delta>\Gamma^{\rm c}_\delta$: with a fixed prize wherever $\Gamma_\delta>0$; with a prize proportional to capacity and neither provision nor diffusion, iff $(1-\beta)r>1$ | Proposition 3.9; Remark 3.10 | $r$ and $\omega$; reflexive channels ($\chi$); patience, with a fixed prize; stakes that do not grow with capacity | Sharing, diffusion and provision through capacity; growth whose return is common; a change of the game, which accurate understanding alone does not make | Both: a narrower working objective raises the temptation and shrinks the sanction, so $\bar p_d$ falls (Proposition 5.17(2))† |
| **Capture** | $d\le d^\ast(\gamma)$: evaluation's row sum at least one, capacity at its selected level; under $[H_\gamma]$, with no per-unit cost floor, a positive cultivation channel and capacity tracking that level, $\alpha_{\rm cor}<\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$, raised by $w\delta_h$ where correction rests on human capacity that delegation erodes | Proposition 4.3; Proposition 4.5; Proposition 4.6; Proposition 4.10; Proposition 4.13 | Appropriability $\chi$ and conversion $b_{ij}$; share and its concentration; a falling cost of control; delegation of corrective work ($\delta_h$); decisiveness, through concentration† | $\chi\to0$; independent correction funded by formula and growing at the rate, assisting human capacity rather than replacing it; structural separation; a per-unit cost floor that AI does not lower | Sanction: captured sanctioners raise $v$, so $\bar p_d$ falls (Proposition 5.17(3))† |
| **Enforcement** | $p_d(a)>\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ for some actor $i$ and deviation $a$ with $D_i(a)>v_i$; where punishment is triggered by detection, $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})<1-\bar p_{d,i}$ | Proposition 5.4; Corollary 5.7; Remark 5.5; Proposition 5.17 | $r$ and $\omega$, through $T_{\rm dec}$†; a gain from deviating and a decisive value that outgrow $C-v$; sharing promised for after victory; captured sanctioners ($\chi$)†; narrow working objectives†; a punishment that is itself a race | Not building the decisive class (E2); common custody; detection and response faster than breakout; objectives under which a decisive position is worth no more than restraint; sanctions that survive success; provision that grows with the stakes, holding $\omega$ | The condition itself |
| **Coverage and tempo** | $\sum_tu_t=\infty$, the uncovered share $u_t=(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$ not summable, under the residual floor; on the race channel, $\sum_th_t=\infty$; for a hazard mode, $a\tau_c\ge1$ | Theorem 5.13; Proposition 5.10; Proposition 5.12; Proposition 5.15 | Capability outrunning contestable coverage, under (variety grows) and (coverage lags) [prem]; manifestation lags $\tau_e$; no point of enough; captured assessment ($\chi$)†; on the race channel, $r$, the stakes and a narrow working objective | Pace bound to understanding (E3); stopping the decisive class, with rollback where variety already exceeds coverage (E2); a point of enough, with bounded hazardous scope and correction that keeps growing; on the race channel, the four levers of Proposition 5.10 | —: it acts whoever races |
| **Talk** | Effective bias $b_0(n-1-\theta)/(n-1+\theta)\ge\frac14$; at $\theta=-1$, always between two actors, and among $n\ge3$ iff $b_0n/(n-2)\ge\frac14$ | Proposition 5.16 | Narrow working objectives†; contested stakes; captured indicators ($\chi$)† | Shared measurement and verification; derivations from public premises and protocols agreed in advance; costly signals; lower contested stakes | Its premise, monitoring: what (14) assumes perfect, uninformative talk does not supply† |
| **Alignment tax** | $(A_\tau)$: countering a divergence lowers the developer's payoff net of every private benefit, under relative cumulative selection among developers, with a matched variant that does not counter it admissible; for a masked divergence, evaluation of propensity cannot show its absence | Proposition 6.7; Lemma 6.5; Remark 6.8 | Competition among developers on relative payoff; a positive tax, class by class (C9); divergences whose costs fall on common terms; masking ($\chi$)†; admission that misses internal deployment | Common admission constraints before consequential deployment, internal deployment included, sanctioned above every type's gain from evasion, and of equal incidence for neutrality among those who comply; for masked classes, compare the tax with expected sanction jointly; capability limits where independent control cannot establish safety, with E2 or E3 beyond them as recommendations under the protection standard [rec]; commonly funded, openly published research that lowers the tax | Temptation: among developers the tax is the gain from dropping a common constraint† |
| **Delegation** | The speed gain from delegating above the delegator's share of the expected control loss; under relative objectives, at any speed | Proposition 6.2; Lemma 6.3; Proposition 6.4 | Speed; relative objectives; an uncertain lock, $q_L<1$; erosion of human capacity ($\delta_h$); stakes large against the shared loss of control | Narrow E1 first, for decisions whose delegation would lock with near certainty, a strict equilibrium without trust among actors who price in the lock; broad E1 as the scope; human capacity kept in practice | Temptation, by conjecture: escalation at machine speed shortens the time detection and response have† [conj] |

Three parameters recur. Decisiveness and the share of capacity that has to be won set compounding, standing and the breakout probability directly, and reach talk and delegation through the working objective that standing sets; the convertibility of success into control enters every row but delegation, most of them through an interface marked †. That is the formal sense in which the conditions are interconnected: not a single pressure, but a few parameters on which several thresholds turn, so that a change in one moves several [thm, cell by cell]; whether they move together in the world, so that growth moves the thresholds together, is C0 [conj]. S7 gives the full matrix of parameters against thresholds, each sign with its intervention, what is held fixed, the model that supplies it and the interface not proved. Three of its entries carry the argument. **In the fixed-prize capacity model, provision not won in the contest lowers the structural gain and local standing price and, through the time a lead needs to become decisive, the breakout probability** (P4; Proposition S2.4, Proposition 3.9, Remark S2.1): the formal content of the image of trees, and the pivot of the scissors (Proposition 5.17). **Patience cuts both ways**, strengthening repetition and, with a fixed prize, raising the price of standing (P19; Section 3.5). **Promised sharing raises the danger in enforcement**, because a decisive winner can break the promise (Proposition 5.8): sharing promised for after victory raises the value of a decisive position by what reneging saves, while sharing as it arises lowers the gain from deviating (P2, P1).

**Four loops.** The author describes the pressure as four loops [corpus: selfishness-spiral], and the rows of Table 7.1 are their formal parts. *Compounding*: "selfish gets rewarded and it compounds, it out grows other behavior patterns", where a frame is selfish when its ledger counts only its own measures, the narrow self-model of Section 3. *Capture of the rules, and adopt or be out*: the successful reach the positions where rules are written, since "NVFs [narrative-value frames] define rules of the game, and the rules of the game determine the rewards of the players", and the rest must follow: "other agents observe them and understand in order to compete with them they also have to follow their rules". *The naturalized narrative*: "as they accumulate more, they create and slowly dominate the narrative", until the pattern is taken "as 'human nature', as granted, as everyone has to follow this because we can not change it". *Regulator lag*: every society keeps a regulator against compounding, its consensus code of norms, laws and shared understanding, which "has to be able to keep up with the pace of change and complexity of the society … in order to keep selfishness in check. (so basically our understanding of the world has to keep up with reality)"; where the world changes faster, the gap is a window in which the first three run unchecked. Three intensifiers act on all four. Paradigms converge: "more and more agents will also becoming single value optimizers through iteration", which removes the plurality on which decorrelated correction depends (Proposition 6.9). Harm and capture reinforce each other, so a crisis becomes a new field of accumulation. And as fewer hold more, more compete over less.

**The formal correspondence.** The four are not alike, and they do not run in series. Compounding and capture are reinforcing channels on one state, the concentration of standing: fed by one win share, their gains add in $\Gamma$ (Proposition 2.2(1)) [thm]. "Adopt or be out" is capture's second direction: a rule whose cost falls on challengers picks winners among the compliant (Lemma 3.3(2), Definition 4.1) [thm]. The naturalized narrative is a reinforcing loop through the formation of minds, a conjecture, since frames are carriers only by hypothesis (Section 5.4) [conj]. Regulator lag is not a fourth reinforcing loop but the balancing loop of correction and mobilization (Remark 4.7), and the first three act on its two properties: capture and the erosion of human capacity lower its gain (Proposition 4.5, Proposition 4.6), while acceleration, masking and alienated language lengthen its delay (Proposition 5.12, Proposition 4.8, Proposition 5.16) [thm, each in its model]. The danger is a shift of dominance from the balancing loop to the reinforcing ones, across delays that run from the turnover of capacity to the formation of minds over generations, which no model here contains (S7 gives the couplings loop by loop). A balancing loop that answers with delay is not a negative term of $\Gamma$ (Section 2.2): at a net gain below one, deviations can still grow, oscillating, so correction that arrives late can destabilize what it is meant to steady (Remark S2.3) [num: CH].

**The drive, the driver and the substrate.** Behind the loops are drives, above all the belief that one is in a struggle for survival, and the fear of falling behind that it produces, which makes every participant faster. This *drive* is not a factor of $\Gamma$: it raises the weight on winning and shortens the horizon, and so narrows the working objective and weakens the repetition on which enforcement rests. Its mechanism is totalization: an actor that holds the belief subordinates every other value to the contest. The author calls the belief an attractor of the meta-attractor: "如果相信自己在生存状态的文化，attractor大多都会totalize，这是一个meta attractor的attractor" (in a culture that believes it is in a state of survival, most attractors totalize; this is an attractor of the meta-attractor) [corpus: meta-attractor]. Part of the drive is made by the loop: a discounted gain near one narrows the working objective as the belief would, and produces the conduct of a struggle for survival in actors who hold no such belief (Proposition 3.9(1)) [thm]; that part is no error of the actor, and only a change of the game removes it (Remark 3.10). Part is held, and whether provision beneath the game lowers it is C8 [conj]. Among persons, the pressure channels the desire for status, a fundamental motive (Anderson, Hildreth and Howland 2015), into dominance rather than prestige, the other route to rank (Henrich and Gil-White 2001) [conj]; a generative order can channel it into prestige for contribution, provided prestige does not convert into control over the shared conditions or their correctors, a condition on convertibility that can be measured (V5; EV16; X1). The *driver* is technology, AI above all, which moves the parameters of every row in either direction (Section 2.4), so that what decides is whether it strengthens the loops more than the counter-loops; the *substrate* is the one host that all carriers share (Section 3).

### 7.2 Contest branches: what the premise of the race can and cannot justify

The three parts of the premise of the race (Section 5.1), (decisive), (fast) and (unsaturated), cut the possible contests into four branches by what a deviation can buy. The first two cuts are the two questions of the enforcement condition: whether a position beyond sanction exists, and whether it arrives before it is answered. Saturation is the third.

**Proposition 7.2 (Classification of contest branches) [thm∣prem; each branch tagged].** Each branch is a set of results already proved, read under that branch's condition. *Let AI be an instrument of the contest.*
1. *Branch I: decisive, fast and unsaturated, so that $p_d\to1$. Where a decisive position is worth at least cooperation, repetition enforces no restraint that is not a stage equilibrium of the objectives held (Proposition 5.4(4)). Restraint is not a best response in the objective to a racing rival when $S_i<P_i$, while joining buys nothing materially in the fully dissipative limit (Proposition 5.1); racing dissipates the prize, the more the more decisive the advantage (Proposition 5.2); delegation becomes dominant as speed grows when its gain exceeds its share of the expected control loss, and is never chosen by actors who price in a certain lock (Proposition 6.2, Proposition 6.4); hazard modes with $a\tau_c\ge1$ are beyond proportional correction unless slowed (Proposition 5.12); and where the side that gains a coming advantage will set the terms (A8), the side that will lose it has a motive for preventive attack wherever fighting now is worth more than peace on those terms (Proposition 5.9) [thm∣prem].*
2. *Branch I′: decisive but saturating, so that advantage converts decisively up to a level above which further advantage changes no outcome. The contest has an object up to that level and none beyond: above it $p_d=0$, and restraint is enforceable by repetition on the terms of Corollary 5.7, while the hazards of development, which do not pass through $p_d$, remain (Theorem 5.13, Proposition 5.10) [thm].*
3. *Branch II: decisive but slow, so that a lead can become decisive but detection and response act within the time it needs with probability $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ near one, and $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$ is small (Remark 5.5). Restraint is enforceable wherever that $p_d$ is at most the bound $\bar p_{d,i}$ of every actor and deviation (Proposition 5.4(2)); a shift in advantage spread over enough periods gives no motive for preventive attack (Proposition 5.9); hazard modes with $a\tau_c<1$ stay within proportional correction (Proposition 5.12); and delegation becomes dominant only at a higher speed, because the speed at which it dominates rises as the loss from defeat falls against the shared loss of control (Proposition 6.2), and a defeat answered before it becomes decisive costs less than one that is not [prem]. Correction and bargaining have time to work [thm∣prem].*
4. *Branch III: not decisive, so that leads do not compound in equilibrium ($\Gamma^{\rm eq}<1$) and no deviation yields a position beyond sanction ($p_d=0$). Intensity, dissipation, shared loss and the race-generated hazard are proportional to decisiveness (Proposition 3.6(1), Proposition 5.2(1), Proposition 5.10): what vanishes as decisiveness falls is the object of the race, not every incentive to compete [thm].*

*Scope.* Each item holds in the model of the result it cites. Membership is set by quantities to be measured, not assumed: the decisive level, the detection lag and the saturating level (C5, C7); a technology can move a contest between branches. Low structural decisiveness does not place a contest in Branch III: channels of capacity and rule support add in $\Gamma$ (Proposition 2.2(1)), and where a lead discourages the follower, rivals who respond can lift the equilibrium gain above one from a structural gain below one (Remark S2.2; S7 gives the cases) [num: LG; MP]. Within Branch I, where a decisive position is worth less than cooperation, cooperation that is not a stage equilibrium can survive $p_d=1$ (Proposition 5.4(4)). *Proof:* S7.

Branch I′ is how nuclear rivalry stabilized once a survivable second strike was secure: the contest kept its object up to that level and lost it beyond, while the hazards of development remained (X11) [obs]. Balancing among rivals supplies a second kind of saturation that does not depend on the technology (X1). Whether advantage in AI saturates is not known (Section 7.3), and Section 11.5 states the consequence conditionally.

The race also selects which winner arrives.

**Proposition 7.3 (What the race selects) [thm∣prem].** The race selects winners by their conduct before victory and, under one premise, by their conduct after it. *Let actors race for a decisive position; let $W$ be the event that an actor wins, $E$ that it does not restrain itself while ahead, and $X$ that, having won, it extends its dominance rather than limiting it.*
1. *If restraint while ahead lowers the probability of winning, $\Pr(W\mid E)>\Pr(W\mid\neg E)$, then $\Pr(E\mid W)>\Pr(E)$ whenever $0<\Pr(E)<1$: winners are selected for not restraining themselves, and repeated rounds compound the shift (Proposition 3.4) [thm].*
2. *If, in addition, conduct before victory and after it are positively correlated, $\Pr(X\mid E)>\Pr(X\mid\neg E)$ [prem], and winning depends on $X$ only through $E$, then $\Pr(X\mid W)>\Pr(X)$: the race shifts winners toward those that extend dominance [thm∣prem].*
3. *No actor can rely on a winner's stated limits on its use of a decisive position, since after a decisive win no one can sanction a broken promise (Proposition 5.8): the commitment problem (Fearon 1995; Powell 2006) [thm].*

*Scope.* The correlation in (2) is a premise, and its base rate is not established (C4; Test I). *Proof:* S7.

A winner is not a point but a field of agencies, firms, laboratories and systems, in which the selection of Sections 2 to 4 continues after victory; Test I measures that field's correctors. Beyond that selection, what restrains a winner is its remaining dependence on the people it governs (Proposition 5.8; C13), and an AI aligned to the winner is not thereby aligned to them (Section 6).

**Corollary 7.4 (What the premise of the race cannot justify) [thm∣prem; val].**
1. *In Branch I, or in Branch II without timely detection, where $S_i<P_i$, racing is each actor's best response in its objective to a racing rival (Proposition 5.1), and repetition restores restraint only where $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ for every actor $i$ and deviation $a$ (Proposition 5.4(2)) [thm].*
2. *The race as a collective course leaves everyone's protection after a win to the winner's objective (Proposition 5.8) and, under the premises of Proposition 7.3(2), shifts winners toward those that extend dominance, and so, where extending dominance goes with giving least (Proposition 5.8), toward those whose objectives give least [thm∣prem].*
3. *The premise of the race, that a rival might win decisively, therefore cannot justify the race as a collective course, including in the form in which the race is safe provided the right actor wins [val: V1–V3].*

*Scope.* Part (3) is a judgment of value resting on V1 to V3 (Section 1), not a derivation: an actor that rejects them can accept (1) and (2) and reject (3). It does not say that an actor is wrong to race given that others race; it says that what each can rationally do cannot be what all should choose. *Proof:* S7.

Where the premise of the race holds, restraint rests on changing the premise, by not building the decisive class, by custody, or by detection faster than breakout, or on objectives under which restraint is already a stage equilibrium (Corollary 5.7); the race supplies neither. The first is the institutional form of the third job of Section 3.7, a selection that counts the system; the second needs objectives wider than accuracy alone supplies (Section 5.1), unless coupling grows with power (C11). In neither case is "we are one" a parameter the result sets. **Oneness is what these requirements are requirements of**: one host, coupled actors, and no outside to absorb the failure. It is a property of the system on every branch; what changes between branches is the institutional form its accurate recognition requires.

The race's own premise is therefore the strongest reason to act before it comes true. Custody, the means it leaves, has to be prepared while leads are short: begun at a warning, it is in place too late wherever building it takes longer than the warning gives. And because the approach of a decisive position is where restraint is least enforceable (Proposition 5.4(4)), a decision to activate custody taken then would face the very condition it is meant to prevent: activation has to be committed in advance and executed by mechanism (Section 12.1, tier 2).

Present evidence suggests that decisive advantage has not arrived: leads are short on every measure, open models trail closed ones by about four months, and algorithmic efficiency diffuses. Whether the lead has changed hands depends on the measure: on a composite index it has stayed with developers in one state, while on a preference leaderboard it is reported to have been traded [obs: EV5, EV9; C5]. Short leads cut both ways. They are consistent with enforcement: the public measures show no breakout beyond punishment, so enforced cooperation may remain available and common custody can still be a preparation, not an emergency. They do not bound the window: public benchmark leads are not a measure of sanctionability, they miss systems held internally, complementary assets and the time balancing needs, and unmeasured internal capability stays in the range of uncertainty like every other unmeasured failure mode. They also hold the contest near parity, which, in contests in which a lead discourages the follower, is where standing is priced highest: summed over the two contestants, the price of standing is higher at parity than at a three-to-one split in every case computed, and total effort is highest at parity (S7 gives the cases) [num: MP; held for outside review]. The window for custody and the hardest-fought stretch of the contest are the same period.

The nearest neighbour of this account in international relations is defensive realism, which it extends by a hazard in development that endangers its holder, and from which it departs by treating self-help as a selected institution rather than a given (Wendt 1992); where offense has the advantage and is hard to tell from defense, AI sits in the doubly dangerous quadrant (Jervis 1978; X1).

### 7.3 Signatures and evidence

Six observable dynamics are signatures of the conditions, each through at least two thresholds of Table 7.1, grouped as: winning sets the objective and the tempo (W); success buys control of correction (C); the shared becomes invisible and cooperation looks naive (N). Their status is read from the register [obs].

| Signature | Thresholds | Status | Register |
|---|---|---|---|
| **T1** Strategic success defined by relative or decisive advantage (W) | Standing; Compounding; Enforcement | Evidenced in declarations, in more than one leading state's documents and more than one developer's statements, with cooperative aims declared in the same documents; conduct shown in one reported case: one reported decision weighed a stated principle against staying at the frontier, and state investors of the kind it weighed took part in the developer's next financing rounds | EV1, EV15 |
| **T2** Capability, deployment and delegation accelerate when rivals advance (W) | Enforcement; Delegation | Evidenced in written commitments, not in conduct: two developers' frameworks condition commitments on rivals; one unreviewed study codes most traced revisions as weakening (Zhu 2026); timing not yet tested | EV2 |
| **T3** Oversight depends on the overseen (C) | Capture; Coverage and tempo, through captured assessment | Partly evidenced: evaluation bodies refocused under voluntary access; pre-emption pursued by litigation, with a negotiated standstill as its first outcome; no impairment of an entrusted purpose shown | EV3 |
| **T4** Success reinvested in influence over evaluation and rules (C) | Capture; Compounding, through rule support | Partly evidenced: large political spending by developers and their backers on both sides of rule-making; its scaling with share and its appropriable part not measured | EV4 |
| **T5** Shared harms not reflected in decisions (N) | Load; Standing | Argued from the structure of the harms; not measured; some harms do return | EV13 (propagation); gap 4 |
| **T6** Restraint and shared provision treated as unrealistic (N) | Enforcement; Talk; Standing | Mixed: dialogue and an incident channel opened and a shared scientific assessment reported, while leading states declined the latest multilateral declaration on military AI and one of them opposed the scientific panel's appointment | EV2, EV7, EV8 |
| Incarnation: tendencies under any developer | Alignment tax (weak incarnation) | Shown in constructed settings, including masked forms; frequencies in deployment unknown | EV6 |
| Incarnation: which tendencies persist, by cost incidence | Alignment tax (the nested loop) | Untested | Test L |
| Decisiveness | Enforcement; Compounding (the branches of Section 7.2) | Not established: leads short on every measure, against (decisive) at present; whether the frontier has changed hands depends on the measure; no evidence either way on saturation | EV5, EV9 |

The signatures sustain one another, the loops of Section 7.1 read at the level of what can be observed: W supplies the success that C converts into control; C removes the correction that would make the shared visible; N makes cooperation look naive, which keeps W in place. T6 is not merely a prejudice: where racing is dominant, or among purely relative actors whose punishments treat them alike (Corollary 5.6), restraint that each would not choose anyway really is unenforceable, and the remedy is a change in what is selected, not a better argument. Nor is it simply correct: where a cooperative equilibrium exists, the belief that restraint will be exploited persists because no one tests it (Section 5.4), and where expectations select among equilibria, a belief that leads are decisive selects one in which they are (Section 3.8) [conj]. In the author's words, the belief that others "will also compete and play this winner takes all game, even if we don't ourselves", so that "not participating means losing", is a thinking that "itself creates the trap and reality" [corpus: selfishness-spiral]. The trajectory is not uniform: seeds of cooperation exist, and whether they become binding and broader in time is the question (Section 11.4). The least measured signature is T4, the link to Section 4, since what Section 4 predicts is appropriable support that scales with share. Capability growth is measured, with the longest-horizon series at the limit of its instrument, while the growth of hazardous behaviour and of residual risk is not, which is why Section 12 treats it as the central crux.

### 7.4 Which layer, and how the diagnosis could fail

The diagnosis is at the layer of mechanism (Definition 2.4(1)), partly evidenced; whether the present configuration is attracting or a long transient is open (Test D). That the six signatures load on one latent factor is a research question, not a registered prediction. What is registered is what the conditions predict and their absorbed components do not (TESTS lists who else predicts each).
- **Test Γ.** $\Gamma$ is not estimated (Section 2.2); in its place, the ratio of conduct's response to shared harm to its response to private cost, which equals $1+\theta$ for the working self-model acted on (Proposition 3.6; S3 gives its confound with how much of the harm is perceived), in the fixed-prize deterministic capacity model should rise with provision not won in the contest relative to the stakes, and with diffusion, holding declared objectives fixed, because both lower the discounted gain and so widen the working objective (Proposition 3.9, Proposition S2.4; S7, P4, gives a computed case) [num: SP]. A fixed relative preference predicts no such interaction.
- **Test E.** Moving one shared parameter should move signatures in more than one group, where a rule aimed at one signature should not. This is the test of C0.
- **Test G.** The gap in restraint between wider and narrower objectives should grow with the breakout probability over the range in which the wider objective still sustains restraint. Where a decisive position is worth more than cooperation it closes as $p_d\to1$, where this theory and structural realism agree, so the test discriminates at intermediate $p_d$ (Section 5.5; S5) [num: JX].

**What would refute it.** Suppose a system moves every threshold of Table 7.1 to its safe side for every capable actor, by the marks that Section 8.2 lists. If the signatures then persist unchanged over the dwell time (Section 11.4), the claim fails. If moving one shared parameter changes only its own signature over that time, the claim that the conditions interlock fails for that parameter. Conversely, durable safety obtained by constraining conduct while the thresholds stay where they were, maintained as stakes and speed grow and classified as such before the outcome is known, would refute the claim that the conditions must change.

**The strongest counter-reading.** AI may be a normal technology: its impacts materialize not as capabilities improve but as they are diffused through the economy, at the pace of institutional adoption (Narayanan and Kapoor 2025); and in the history of general-purpose technologies, great-power advantage has come from the capacity to diffuse them, not from a lead at the frontier (Ding 2024). On either reading a lead at the frontier is not decisive in itself. Where deployment is slow enough for contestable coverage to keep up, (coverage lags) fails class by class (C1); where no advantage is decisive, the contest is in Branch III and $p_d=0$ (Proposition 7.2; C5). Both are measurable, and the judgment registers what follows if they are measured favourable (Section 12.3); the levers of Part I remain, diffusion among them (Proposition S2.4). X8 meets the policy side of the reading.

If the diagnosis holds, the question is what would change it. Part III compares paths by one standard, and takes two things from this section: Table 7.1, as the test of what a path changes, and the status column of Section 7.3, as the evidence that the range of uncertainty over which every path is judged must respect.

# Part III. Responses: what would change the trajectory

Part III's standard of comparison is a judgment of value, its descriptions of arrangements are definitions, and its formal results hold under the premises each states; which whole design to prefer is left to Part IV.

## 8. Comparing paths, and the alternatives on their strongest terms

> **Claim.** Paths are compared by one standard: irreversible losses first, one range of uncertainty for every path, each path's own failure modes included; no path has yet been shown to meet the tolerance, and without comparative risk bounds the ranking of whole designs remains a judgment under that standard [val]. An arrangement is described by which thresholds of Table 7.1 it moves, to which side and for whom, and whether its enforcement survives a decisive position [def]. Where an arrangement's hazard is no higher than continuation's, both after it dissolves into its fallback and while it lasts, its own capture and collusion included, it survives at least as well at every horizon, whatever decides its dissolution [thm]. That arrangements which constrain conduct while leaving the thresholds where they were erode as stakes and speed grow, and that those which move them for every capable actor persist, is a registered prediction [conj].
>
> **Why.** Every arrangement that changes the map from conduct to share, resources and standing changes selection, so labels decide nothing [def]. Met at their strongest, the alternatives that keep the contest leave thresholds where they were, each by a mechanism shown in its own model: a lead decays and is judged by its holder; a winner's restraint rests on its need for the governed; a sprint's freeze binds all but its holder, the largest conversion target; verification bounds defection and leaves the value of winning in place [thm∣prem].

### 8.1 The standard of comparison

**The protection standard [val].** Each candidate path is evaluated at common horizons, near (about five years), medium (about twenty) and long (fifty or more), over one justified range $\Theta$ of uncertain parameters applied to every path. First, bound the probability of O1 and O2 within each horizon over $\Theta$. Second, among paths whose bound stays below a stated tolerance, prefer the one with greater useful benefit, more fairly distributed, that keeps more options of correction open. Third, if no path meets the tolerance, prefer the one with the lowest bound that keeps options open. This is robust satisficing on irreversible losses, a normative choice; the criteria it declines rank paths otherwise, and each disagreement is a normative crux. Expected value can favour a small probability of a very large loss when the expected benefit is large. Under discounted expected utility the acceptable existential risk turns on how fast the value of a year of life rises with consumption (Jones 2024; S8 gives his figures) [obs: EV11]; with utility linear in consumption, so that it does not rise, a course is preferred whenever the hazard it adds is below the growth it adds, though it ends in catastrophe with certainty (S8) [thm]. O1 protects catastrophic, common-cause losses of life, which the standard does not discount; deaths from benefits forgone are a cost of the path, not O1, their weight is the normative crux N1, and the margin that only the decisive class could supply is crux C10.

**The range $\Theta$ [val].** A parameter value enters $\Theta$ if it is consistent with the dated evidence of the register and is produced by a stated mechanism whose premises are named, with a precedent or base rate where one exists; a value that needs a premise the register contradicts is excluded. The rule is the same for every path: a rising uncovered share enters on continuation, its premises being named mechanisms the register does not contradict (EV5–EV7), and every other path's failure modes enter on that path, the capture of a common custodian, dissolution, coercive enforcement, interrupted services and collusion among checks on the cooperative one (X8). Every entry cites its source.

**Definition 8.1 (The correctable kernel) [def].** *The correctable kernel is the set of states of the world from which some non-anticipative strategy of admissible correction avoids O1 and O2 against every sequence of errors in the justified range $\Theta$: a union over strategies of correction and an intersection over sequences of error. In viability theory it is a discriminating, or guaranteed-viability, kernel of a game against disturbances (Cardaliaguet 1996; Cardaliaguet, Quincampoix and Saint-Pierre 1999), not the viability kernel of undisturbed dynamics.*
