Leaving the kernel is the loss of guaranteed correctability, a point of no guaranteed return earlier than the losses themselves. The corpus describes openness by four capacities, each a condition of the kernel [corpus: possibility-space]: *not yet exhausted* (未), no decisive end state reached; *still possible* (可), plurality of lineages, purposes and correctors kept; *still responsive* (应), humans able to suspend, modify or replace; *still able to turn back* (转), reversal possible. A closure made to protect openness, such as a defence, a quarantine or an emergency power, can state what would make it withdraw; one that cannot has left the kernel. The corpus names the two: 护闭, closing one place for a time to protect a larger opening, and 成闭, a closing that does not reopen [corpus: possibility-space]. The standard's preference for keeping options of correction open is a preference for staying inside the kernel. This paper computes no kernel; Theorem 10.4 computes a smaller thing, a boundary of viable parameters under one fixed strategy.

**Lemma 8.2 (When an arrangement cannot do worse) [thm].** Two design conditions guarantee that an arrangement does no worse than continuation at any horizon. *Let an arrangement $A$ and continuation $C$ be driven by the same exogenous world process, the irreversible event on each path occurring at date $t$ when a common uniform draw falls below that path's conditional hazard given the shared history. Suppose that on every history on which both paths have survived to $t$: (safe fallback) once $A$ has dissolved, its hazard is no higher than $C$'s; and (bounded own risk) while $A$ lasts, its hazard, its own hazards of capture, restriction and collusion included, is no higher than $C$'s. Then $A$'s survival probability is at least $C$'s at every horizon, whatever the rule by which $A$ dissolves, rules that depend on the state included.*

*Scope.* Where bounded own risk fails there is no pointwise dominance, and the standard compares worst cases; that is where regulated competition can do better. *Proof:* S8, which also shows that comparing after conditioning on the dissolution date fails when dissolution depends on the state [num: SM(4), SM(14), FD].

Both conditions are read from an arrangement's own documents; the first is the durability test D1 of Definition 8.4. An agreement whose fallback is a short sprint from preserved relative standing violates the first; a design whose own concentration, capture or collusion can exceed continuation's hazards violates the second, and whether the design of Section 10 does is crux C4.

Comparing by survival does not by itself make restraint an equilibrium. Write $\Phi^R$, $\Phi^T$, $\Phi^P$ and $\Phi^S$ for an actor's probability of surviving to a common horizon when all restrain, when it races against restrainers, when all race, and when it restrains against a racer, each counting the hazards of its branch. The signs of $\Phi^R-\Phi^T$ and $\Phi^S-\Phi^P$ make the game in survival terms dominance, a stag hunt, chicken or a prisoner's dilemma; mutual racing is a strict equilibrium at every horizon at which $\Phi^P>\Phi^S$; and where the arrangement's own survival also decays, signs that hold at near and medium horizons can reverse at long ones (Proposition S8.1) [thm].

So paths are compared at common finite horizons, not at an infinite one at which both branches of a hazardous world can tend to zero. Under the rule for $\Theta$, continuation with strong correction and a design that changes selection both admit irreversible-loss branches. That fact alone does not bound their probabilities or show that either exceeds the tolerance. No path has yet been shown to meet it, and the bounds needed to apply the standard's third step are unestimated; Section 12.1 therefore states a provisional judgment over the failure modes of every path that X8 lists.

### 8.2 Profiles, not labels

**Definition 8.3 (What an arrangement changes) [def].** *An arrangement is described by a profile: which mechanisms of Part I it neutralizes, weakens, leaves or strengthens, read as the thresholds of Table 7.1 it moves and the side to which it moves them; its coverage (unilateral, plurilateral, every capable actor); its bindingness (Abbott et al. 2000); whether its enforcement survives a decisive position, and if it prevents one from arriving, by what verified mechanism; whether its beneficiaries can amend it; its fallback and own risk; and its durability. And selection: every arrangement that changes the map from conduct to share, resources and standing changes selection, so what is recorded is the direction, whether it moves the thresholds toward their safe side or away from it, and whether in every capable actor's game or only in rivals'.*

Export controls aimed at preserving a lead change selection, toward a lead: blocking diffusion to rivals raises the structural loop gain in the channels it reaches, the gain the lead is meant to exploit (Section 2.2). At illustrative values, with nothing shared, no provision and diffusion symmetric, blocking it moves the threshold at which leads compound from a decisiveness of about 3 to 6 to any decisiveness above one (Remark S2.5); targeted controls block diffusion only in part, and to rivals only, which the symmetric computation approximates. X8 profiles the policies now in force.

**The generative direction.** A change of selection in the generative direction has seven marks:
- **G1** consequences return to their source;
- **G2** winning confers reward, not more advantage in the same game;
- **G3** success cannot buy control over its own assessment, and correction stays plural, decorrelated and in common hands;
- **G4** decisive capability is held in common or not built;
- **G5** provision of the conditions of a decent life sits beneath the game;
- **G6** measurement and language are shared;
- **G7** pace is bound to understanding.

Each reverses thresholds of Table 7.1, in the model of the row it reverses [thm, row by row]: G1 load, and the alignment tax where admission returns a divergence's common cost to its developer; G2 and G5 together compounding and standing, since non-convertible reward works only with provision not won in the contest, and G5 holds down the won share, the pivot of the scissors (Proposition 5.17), where the provision it names enters contestants' decisive stocks, sense (a) of Section 2.2, acting otherwise through the drive (C8) [conj]; G3 capture; G4 enforcement, by keeping $p_d$ small for every deviation (Corollary 5.7); G6 talk; G7 coverage and tempo, the capability bound for masked classes, and, applied to decisions, delegation, which narrow E1 keeps within the pace of human correction. G2 is the author's rule of game design (Section 2.2), and the seven are what Section 3.7 asks of a selection that counts the system. What this paper argues for is a change of selection with these marks, covering every capable actor, enforceable and durable, whatever it is called.

**Definition 8.4 (Durability tests) [def].** *An arrangement is tested on its own documents and estimates, not on assumptions added by the tester: D1, its fallback if it dissolves; D2, its estimated hazard of breakdown and what drives it; D3, who keeps informed veto, modification, replacement and exit after any delegation; D4, whether its own correctors have full inbound row sums below one; D5, what happens when its strongest participant violates it; D6, succession and the continuity of essential services; D7, common causes and collusion among its checks and systems; D8, whether membership is open or stops at a club.*

The tests apply to every path, the cooperative design of Section 10 included, by one evidence standard: where an arrangement's documents give no entry, the entry says so (X8). An arrangement with the form of cooperation that fails D1–D3 on its own estimates may be called a *pseudo-solution*; the label summarizes test results and does not replace them. **Registered prediction [conj]:** arrangements that constrain conduct while leaving the thresholds of Table 7.1 where they were erode as stakes and speed grow, because the mechanisms of Sections 3 to 5 keep acting on them; those that move the thresholds to their safe side for every capable actor persist, because what would erode them has been removed. The classification uses the profile, never the outcome; Section 7.4 states what would refute it.

### 8.3 The alternatives

Each alternative is met in its strongest form, on its merits rather than its prominence, and answered by mechanism: what remains, and which thresholds it leaves. X8 gives each in full, with how it would be installed and what would show that it suffices.

**(a) Technical alignment and AI-assisted correction.** *Strongest form:* control over early transformative systems, used to accelerate alignment and oversight while the window lasts (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan and Roger 2024); oversight with the right topology (Proposition 6.9); AI-assisted correction keeping pace with cheapening control (Proposition 4.5); research that drives the alignment tax below zero, class by class, so that the safe variant is also the winning one, or to zero, where it is no longer selected against (Section 6.4); and, in its proponents' own plans, a strong international agreement ranked first. *What remains:* principal alignment does not change the game among principals (Proposition 6.1); the window lasts only as long as the race allows; systems are retained for performance (Lemma 6.5), and progress that lowers the hazard per unit of racing or delegation is partly absorbed by more of both (Proposition S6.2); redundant oversight is capped by the common causes selection produces, and certified row sums are a sufficient certificate, not a proof of independent errors (Proposition 6.9); AI-assisted correction is only as independent as its funding, access, appointment and lineage; time is a part of the tax that no technique removes; for masked classes, deterrence fails where detection times the enforceable sanction falls below the tax, including at sufficiently low detection under a fixed positive tax; a falling tax must be compared jointly (C9, C12). Missing assurance supports a capability bound under the protection standard, without proving an unavoidable residual hazard (Remark 6.8); and which divergences are tolerated is set at the outermost loop (Proposition 6.7). Held by correctors separated from success, under constraints common to all, it changes selection, as part of G3 and G7.

**(b) Regulated competition with strong correction.** *Strongest form:* liability and licensing make consequences return, evaluation access is mandated and thresholds are fixed in advance; in a two-developer race with public capability, sufficiently high effective hazard sustains a pacing Markov perfect equilibrium and excludes the everywhere-racing profile, without proving uniqueness or pacing from every initial state (Fudenberg and Koh 2026); civil nuclear power and aviation show durable safety under unchanged commercial motives [obs]. *What remains:* the race is set by the most race-inclined actor (Proposition 5.1); the rule-makers are themselves in a contest of standing (Proposition 9.2); liability cannot exceed the assets of the liable (Shavell 1986), so a catastrophic or common harm cannot be turned wholly into a private cost; developers' frameworks condition commitments on rivals [obs: EV2]; the precedents had no decisive prize; and the mechanisms here can weaken the incentives and information on which pacing rests: relative accounting discounts shared harm (Proposition 3.5), while quiet deviation, captured evaluation and masking obstruct its assessment (Proposition 5.4, Proposition 4.8, Remark 6.8); their mapping into the source model remains to be shown (X8). Regulation that returns consequences and separates evaluators from success, across every capable actor, is G1 and G3 for all, the change this paper argues for, whatever it is called; regulation that constrains conduct while success can still buy the rule-makers is predicted to erode as stakes and speed grow.

**(c) Leadership and a safety margin.** *Strongest form:* a leader spends part of its lead on safety; a large lead can discourage a follower (Harris and Vickers 1987; Konrad and Kovenock 2009); and a developer able to commit first to a slightly slower speed gains, because rivals slow too, a local result that needs no lead (Bueno de Mesquita, Dziuda and Polborn 2026). *What remains* (Remark S8.2): a lead shrinks by diffusion even when the follower does not accelerate; spending it gives up the discouragement and returns the contest toward parity, where, in contests in which a lead discourages the follower, standing is priced highest (Section 7.2); for hazard classes whose evidence lag exceeds the margin, it buys nothing (Proposition 5.12); whether it suffices is judged inside the contest, by the leader's own evaluators; and a leader whose window is closing has a motive to strike first. It acts on tempo for a time and leaves the objective of winning in place; its best case, a first commitment to slow that rivals answer by slowing, is a first step of the transition (Section 11), not a substitute for it.

**(d) The benign winner: win, bind, share.** *Strongest form:* a coalition seeks a clear advantage, including military superiority and the denial of key inputs to adversaries, then offers its benefits to others in exchange for their support (EV1). It persuades by promising both security and benefits; both rest on the winner's restraint after the fact. By this paper's own results the form is strongest where the winner still needs the governed: under logarithmic utility a winner accurate toward what can be divided gives them $\kappa_W/(1+\kappa_W)$ (Section 5.2). *What remains:* that need after victory is what the race cannot verify beforehand, and what AI can lower (C13); after a decisive win what the others receive is set by the winner's objective alone, and a promise to share is worth breaking, with no one to sanction it (Proposition 5.8) [thm]; where conduct before and after victory go together, the race shifts winners toward those that extend dominance (Proposition 7.3) [thm∣prem]; and the winner is itself a field, whose restraint depends on whether its own correctors can be bought by its successes. A winner that binds what victory confers, shares benefits as they arise rather than promising them, and has its own correctors certified independent, with succession rules that survive changes of leadership (Test I), has changed what it selects for.

**(e) A finite sprint, then restraint.** *Strongest form:* sprint to a decisive lead, then freeze or roll back and govern at the tempo of understanding; a winner that freezes keeps positive survival (Proposition 5.14), and after a decisive win no contest remains to price standing. *What remains:* the path passes through its greatest exposure, and the rivals' strongest motive for preventive attack (Proposition 5.9), before any restraint begins (C1, C5); and a decisive advantage not used to stop others' growth does not stay decisive, while using it so ends their O2 (V2, V3; Remark S8.3). The paper's own answer is by mechanism: the freeze is a stop one actor imposes on all the others, the most unequal incidence a constraint can have, so it re-sorts those it binds and is evaded wherever evasion pays their type (Lemma 3.3(2), (3)) [thm], and they read it as a lead being kept; and its holder is the largest conversion target there is, the return to influence over correctors growing with the square of share (Proposition 4.3) [thm]. E2 and common custody impose the same stop on the decisive class; what separates the sprint from them is incidence and holder. The sprinter's freeze answers to its own correctors and, when the sprinter breaks it, to no one (D4, D5); custody is designed for separated functions under correctors certified independent of every party, its certificates not yet measured (C4; X8 sets the two side by side).

**(f) Deterrence and prevention.** Prevention can be survival-preferred by the preventer.

**Proposition 8.5 (Prevention under the preventer's own criterion) [thm].** *Let an actor compare courses by its own survival at a common horizon, facing a rival it believes certain to race. (1) It strictly prefers a single strike, which escalates to catastrophe with probability $\varepsilon_P$ and otherwise succeeds with probability $s_P$ in returning the field to something like the arrangement, leaving the race as it was if it fails, to both staying out and joining iff $\varepsilon_P<1-\max(\Phi^S,\Phi^P)/\big(s_P\Phi^R+(1-s_P)\Phi^P\big)$; this bound tends to one as the racing branches $\Phi^S$ and $\Phi^P$ tend to zero, wherever $s_P\Phi^R>0$, and rises when they fall in proportion. (2) Sustained prevention against a target that rebuilds with probability at least $b_P$ a period, each strike escalating with probability at least $\varepsilon_{\min}$, has a per-period hazard of at least $b_P\varepsilon_{\min}$.*

*Scope.* Prevention that permanently ends the target's capacity, or converts into joint monitoring, is a different branch. *Proof:* S8, which also compares sustained prevention with racing at common horizons [num: legacy v5, FS].

The objections are three, and none changes the criterion: normatively, prevention ends or endangers others' O1 and O2 (V2, V3); epistemically, it can make a rival that was in fact survival-first act as the type it was feared to be (Jervis 1976; Kydd 1997); technically, striking at machine speed against unobservable progress brings the delegation of Section 6.3. Deterrence by threatened sabotage (Hendrycks, Schmidt and Wang 2025) acts on $p_d$ by threat, as custody does by holding the decisive class, with war or race as its fallback; its credibility can reverse under survival-first comparisons, its red lines are hard to state and to monitor (X8), and algorithmic progress erodes thresholds stated in compute [obs: EV9].

Insuring against falling behind is not an exit either: the penalty is set by the winner, not the laggard, and insurance accepts the frame of the contest, hedging against losing instead of declining the game (Remark S8.4). That is the case the ruling of Section 3.8 closes: against a winner of that logic falling behind cannot be made survivable, so a remedy has to oppose the logic, not hedge inside it. Provision held by no one, beneath the game, lowers the won share for every actor (G5); held by one actor as insurance against losing, it is a position in the contest, which a winner with a relative objective has a motive to destroy.

**The hard case.** A survival-first actor that gives a low probability to a capable rival's holding an unbounded relative objective (Section 3.8) does best to propose verifiable limits and to take narrow E1 verifiably in its own strategic loops, a costly signal (Proposition 11.1). With a high probability it compares denial, a coalition that stops the decisive class, and prevention, and coverage of every capable actor then comes from consent or from coercion. Coercive denial raises the weight on winning and the trust threshold and, if it escalates, falls under Proposition 8.5. The theory does not derive that its net effect is positive and states the case as hard; Section 12.1 states, once, the conditions under which a coercive restriction of frontier inputs enters even the comparison of designs.

**(g) A verified agreement over a contest that remains.** Rivals agree to slow and to delay decisive capability under verification, with mutually assured destruction of compute if the agreement dissolves (X8). Let the payoffs of mutual restraint, of racing against a restrainer, of mutual racing and of restraining against a racer be $R$, $T$, $P$ and $S$; let verification detect a defection in time with probability $\upsilon$, in which case it is reversed, the defector receiving $P-F$ and the cooperator $P$; let trust $\varpi$ be the belief that the other cooperates; and write $g_C=R-\upsilon(P-F)-(1-\upsilon)T$ for what cooperating gains against a cooperator and $g_D=(1-\upsilon)(P-S)$ for what it loses against a defector.

**Proposition 8.6 (Assurance) [thm].** *In this symmetric two-player game: (1) cooperation is a best response iff $\varpi\ge g_D/(g_C+g_D)$ when both are positive; no trust suffices if $g_C\le0<g_D$; cooperation is dominant if $g_D\le0<g_C$. (2) Where $T-P+F>0$ and the value of winning exceeds the value of cooperating, $T>R$, cooperating against a cooperator pays iff $\upsilon>(T-R)/(T-P+F)$; where $T<R$, it pays without verification.*

*Scope.* The threshold of (2) is the deterrence condition of the inspection game (Avenhaus, von Stengel and Zamir 2002); read with its payoffs as continuation values, it is, in the one-period reduction, the enforcement condition seen from verification, $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d}$ (Remark 5.5). *Proof:* S8, with the $n$-player bounds and the condition under which they fail.

**Theorem 8.7 (Verification and the value of winning) [thm].** Verification is required exactly when, and to the degree that, the value of winning exceeds the value of cooperating.
1. *For a fixed sanction, the timely detection that cooperation needs, $(T-R)/(T-P+F)$, tends to one as $T$ grows.*
2. *Detection counts only if it comes before a defection becomes decisive, so that $p_d=1-\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})$; acceleration, which shortens $T_{\rm dec}$, lowers the timely detection that any given instrument achieves.*
3. *Verification can also lower $T$ by bounding the largest defection that would go undetected, for instance by accounting for compute, which does not need near-certain detection. The bound holds while the verified coalition's advantage in effective compute stays above the ratio at which an undetected project would become decisive [def]; since effective compute is algorithmic efficiency times compute, the coalition's compute advantage must then grow at least as fast as diffusion erodes its algorithmic one. That it does, the premise (advantage keeps pace), is not derived here [conj], and the rate of erosion is observed (EV9).*

*Scope.* Verification does not carry unverifiable information (Proposition 5.16), its own reliability (Section 4), or future commitments, unless the sanction keeps pace with the net gain from exit, which grows with the capability held in check when leaving with a decisive advantage gains faster than staying with one's share; an agreement that preserves relative positions preserves the relative term of every payoff, and with it the motive to exit (Proposition S8.5) [thm]. *Proof:* S8.

One detailed plan of this kind among leading powers, designed to be enforceable without trust (EV10; X8), stands for a position, keep the contest, slow it and stay safe, and the answer above is to the position, not to the plan's figures. It slows and caps, acting on tempo and decisiveness, and keeps relative standing in its enforcement and fallback, and with it the motive to exit. That makes it a bridge, and it can be the end only if it lowers the value of winning, as G2 and G5 do, rather than only bounding defection, as (3) of Theorem 8.7 does.

**(h) Normal technology and resilience.** *Strongest form:* AI's impacts arrive as it diffuses, at the pace of adoption, and policy should seek resilience and reject nonproliferation, which lowers it (Narayanan and Kapoor 2025); great-power advantage from a general-purpose technology comes from diffusing it, not from a lead at the frontier (Ding 2024). *What remains:* the growth channel binds at the pace of deployment, and whether coverage keeps up is measured class by class (C1); whether any advantage is decisive is C5, and where both are measured favourable the activation of tier 2 lapses (Section 12.3); the concentration that custody risks is answered by the two orders of Remark 10.3 and the guardian test (Remark 10.5); and the reading's own prescriptions, resilience, diffusion and shared measurement, are G1, G3 and G6 and the diffusion lever of Section 2.2. X8 gives it in full.

### 8.4 The comparison

| Path | What it changes | Thresholds of Table 7.1 it leaves on the danger side | Fallback (D1) | Crux |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Technical alignment and AI-assisted correction alone | Residual harm per unit; tempo; the alignment tax, where its reduction is published | Compounding; standing; enforcement; the tax for masked classes | The race | C1, C3, C9, C12 |
| Regulated competition within jurisdictions | Load and capture (G1, G3), within one jurisdiction | Enforcement and compounding across jurisdictions; capture of the rule-makers in their own contest | The race | C2, EV2 |
| Leadership and margin | Tempo, for a time | Compounding; standing, highest at the parity to which a spent lead returns; enforcement | The race, at parity | C5 |
| The benign winner | Nothing before victory | Enforcement, replaced after victory by the winner's objective; capture inside the winner | Domination | C4, C13, Test I |
| Finite sprint, then restraint | Tempo, after the sprint | During the sprint, enforcement and coverage; after it, load, by the most unequal incidence, and capture, by the largest conversion target | Prevention | C1, C5 |
| Deterrence by sabotage | $p_d$, by threat | Compounding; standing; delegation, which militarization pushes toward danger | War or race | C7 |
| Coercive restriction of frontier inputs (compared in Section 12.1 under its own conditions) | $p_d$, by denial; the weight on winning, raised | Load, by unequal incidence unless the restrictor bears it too; capture, the restrictor being the largest conversion target | Escalation (Proposition 8.5) | C2, C7 |
| Verified agreement over a remaining contest | Tempo and decisiveness, bounded | Standing and compounding, relative positions preserved | A short sprint from preserved standing | C5, C7 |
| Normal technology and resilience, without nonproliferation | Diffusion and resilience, against concentration | Coverage and tempo where deployment outruns contestable coverage (C1); enforcement where an advantage proves decisive (C5) | The race, at the pace of diffusion | C1, C5 |
| A design changing selection (Section 10) | The shared parameters, for every capable actor, by G1–G7 | None by design; whether it holds them is C4, and its own hazards enter $\Theta$ | Separately held functions (Section 10.5) | C4, C9 |

The comparison turns on two quantities. If continuation keeps the uncovered hazardous share summable, it can beat a fragile cooperative arrangement; if the uncovered share rises on the continuation path, any cooperative arrangement with a modest hazard of dissolution does better at every horizon past the near term [num: legacy v5, CMP; S8]. The first is crux C1; the second is what Sections 10 and 11 design for and test. X8 gives each path's main residual risk and, path by path, each failure mode in $\Theta$: whether and into what it can be reversed, whether that is derived or only designed, and what would show it under way, the base on which Section 12.1 compares. The analysis does not establish that every cooperative arrangement outperforms regulated competition, and Section 12 does not claim it.

Section 9 states what any adequate arrangement must do to move the thresholds for every capable actor, whatever it is called, taking from here the profile and the marks G1–G7; Section 12 takes the durability tests, the failure modes of X8 and the third step of the standard.

## 9. What any adequate arrangement must do

> **Claim.** What every adequate arrangement must do can be stated before designs are compared, and any design, regulated competition included, checked against it: each requirement with its kind of support (necessary in a named model over a named horizon, sufficient, or normative) and with the conditions under which it does not bind [def]. Where (reach) holds with a race carrier present, and no single rule-maker reaches every carrier whose load can carry the host below its floor, several requirements cannot be met by any actor alone, and competition among actors does not supply them [thm].
>
> **Why.** A constraint that one carrier adopts alone is a cost borne by it alone, which relative selection removes while effective liability is below the private advantage; a rule-maker leaves the host to the carriers it does not reach; and rule-makers in a contest of standing, weighing it above a stated level, do not impose a liability alone [thm]. Every sanction, observation and count of coverage that the rows call for is a corrector's act [def], and an enacted liability binds only in the part of rule-making that success has not bought [thm].

Part I proved its results one model at a time, and Table 7.1 reads them as thresholds, each with a side on which a protected outcome is lost. Read in reverse, such a result is a requirement: an arrangement that does not keep the field on the safe side loses the outcome, in that model and over that horizon. The kind of support decides how a row may be used: a necessary row is a test, which a design that lacks it fails in the row's model within its horizon; a sufficient row is a route, not a test; a normative row rests on a value of Section 1.3 and is declared, not derived. The means by which a design meets a row are not rows; they are the subject of Section 10. Nor are sharing, provision beneath the game and reward that does not convert into control, which the models give as alternatives, any one of which can carry the margin that keeps equal standing stable (Proposition 2.3) [thm]; they are the levers of Section 10.2, each meeting several rows at once (S9, under the crosswalk). Read together, the rows state with their models the third job of "we are one" (Section 3.7), a selection that counts the system. S9 derives each row, fixes its scope with counterexamples, and maps the rows onto the paper's other lists (Table S9.2).

**Table 9.1 (Requirements, by the kind of support each has) [def, with support: each row carries the tag of the result it cites].** *Each row holds only in the model and over the horizon of its third column. The last column names the routes by which an arrangement does without the row, and marks what meets it rather than excusing it. Coverage is of actors in R2 and of hazard modes in R5 (Section 5.3). The table organizes results proved elsewhere and ranks no design.*

| Requirement | Kind | Needed for, in which model | Basis | Not needed when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **R1** Consequences return: long-run effective liability at least the private advantage of load, $\limsup_t\frac1t\int_0^t\tau_{\rm eff}\,ds\ge\nu$; sharper, a cumulative advantage of load $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\,ds$ that does not tend to infinity | necessary [thm] | viability at every horizon; the host (3), cumulative selection (Definition 3.2) among carriers otherwise alike, a race carrier present, (reach) | Proposition 3.4; Theorem 10.4(3) | a horizon that ends before the floor is reached; (reach) fails; other advantages correlated with restraint offset the load (the scope of Proposition 3.4) |
| **R2** Coverage: the common constraints reach every actor whose share can grow | necessary [thm] | the same, with a carrier the constraints do not reach; a race that begins if any one capable actor prefers it | Proposition 3.4; Proposition 5.1; Lemma 3.3(2) | harms that stay local; uncovered carriers whose combined share stays too small for their load, with what the covered carriers still load, to carry the host below its floor, $h_V[\bar p_{\rm unc}\bar e+(1-\bar p_{\rm unc})\bar e_{\rm cov}]<\rho(1-V_{\min})$; a horizon shorter than the crossing time |
| **R3a** Independent correction certified at the start and growing at least at $\alpha_{\rm ctl}/(1+\eta)$, faster by $w\delta_h$ where it rests on human capacity that delegation erodes | sufficient at any speed of adaptation, for every adjustment of capacity that starts at or below its selected level and does not raise it while above [thm]; necessary where capacity tracks its selected level, $\liminf_tq/q^\ast>0$ [thm] | the evaluation certificate at every date; the field model with a seeded evaluation domain, a positive cultivation channel, $[H_\gamma]$ and no per-unit cost floor | Proposition 4.5(2); Proposition 4.6(1) | $\chi=0$ or the conversion channels removed (Proposition 4.5(1)), or a per-unit cost floor that AI does not lower (Proposition 4.5(3)), where a level of correction suffices; a floor that AI lowers more slowly than correction grows, for the certificate from some date on; capacity selected gradually, its variance not rising faster than capacity, for the certificate in the end (Proposition S4.2); nothing captured; a horizon shorter than the failure time |
| **R3b** No core of mutually sustaining capture among the corrector domains | necessary [thm] | no capture from every initial state; the field model | Proposition 4.10(4) | only the states actually reached need be excluded |
| **R4** Breakouts within the enforceable bound: $p_d(a)\le\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ for every actor $i$ and every deviation $a$ with $D_i(a)>v_i$; where punishment is triggered by detection, $\upsilon(T_{\rm dec})\ge1-\bar p_{d,i}(a)$ | necessary [thm] | restraint enforced by repetition under perfect monitoring, where a deviation can reach a decisive position worth more than the punishment | Proposition 5.4(2); Corollary 5.7; Remark 5.5 | no deviation reaches a position beyond punishment, so that $p_d=0$ and ordinary repetition decides (Branch III; Branch I′ above its saturating level; Proposition 7.2); $(1-\delta)g_i\le\delta(C_i-D_i)$ for every actor and deviation, that is $\bar p_{d,i}\ge1$, which $D_i<C_i$ alone does not give (Corollary 5.7). Detection and response faster than breakout meet R4; they do not excuse it |
| **R5** A summable uncovered hazardous share in each hazard class, $\sum_tu_t<\infty$ with $u_t=(1-U_t/Y_t)^+$ | necessary under (residual floor) [thm∣prem]; sufficient where residual risk is at most $\bar hu_t$ with $\bar h<1$ [thm] | survival not tending to zero as the horizon grows; every actor's under (shared failure), whoever is racing | Lemma 5.11; Theorem 5.13 | (residual floor) fails, as under correction whose blocking becomes complete (Proposition S6.4); a finite horizon, where only the exposure up to it counts (Proposition 5.14). Met, not excused, where hazardous variety stops growing at a level set by need while coverage keeps growing (Proposition 5.15) |
| **R6** Observation independent of the observed, with any bound on masking drawn from evidence the observed controls less directly; shared measurement; verifiable signals | sufficient [thm] | alarms that fire no later than the condition they watch reaches its target; trust without good will | Remark 4.9; Proposition 4.8; Proposition 5.16 | channels that observe actions directly, for what they observe; for trust, stakes on the topic of talk that are not contested |
| **R7** Standing for those affected; inclusion that does not stop at a club | normative [val] | V3 | Proposition 3.12, which shows harm to outsiders over-produced, not that O1 or O2 is reached | never, for the normative part |
| **R8a** Common admission constraints before consequential deployment, internal deployment included, for divergences whose costs fall on common terms, with an expected sanction at least the gain from evasion for every type that can evade; decisions retained by contest performance only inside them | necessary [thm∣prem]; the sanction condition necessary within a type and, across types, sufficient to keep the divergence from spreading, and to remove it under cumulative selection where every inequality is strict [thm] | relative, cumulative selection among developers under $(A_\tau)$, a matched variant admissible; decisions retained by contest performance | Proposition 6.7(3); Lemma 6.5; Lemma 3.3(3) | developers selected by something other than relative payoff, such as a public mandate that does not merely move the selection to the level of states; a tax zero or negative for the class (C9), as a liability for harm that can be attributed can make it, the route of R1; decisions not retained by contest performance |
| **R8b** Equal incidence of the common constraints among those who comply | necessary and sufficient for neutrality among compliers [thm]; required by V5 [val] | a rule that neither picks winners among those who comply nor bars their challengers; payoff-monotone selection, costs additive | Lemma 3.3(2); Definition 4.1 | not needed to keep a divergence out: unequal incidence re-sorts compliers without letting it back in |
| **R9** Human decision authority kept: where delegation would erode the capacity to take consequential decisions back, that capacity kept above its floor in practice, or the decisions not delegated past the lock | necessary [thm∣prem] | O2 in each such domain; capacity that decays under delegation and recovers slowly without it, in a contest that denies the time to recover | Lemma 6.3; Proposition 6.4 | delegation that leaves the capacity intact; automation bounded and reversible within the time available, as the test of O2 decides (Section 6.6). Met without an arrangement where every actor prices in a lock it knows to be certain (Proposition 6.4(1)) |

**The row the others rest on.** Every row but R7 rests on the work of correctors [def]. A liability binds at $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, what captured rule-making leaves of it (Section 4.1); the sanctions that bound breakouts and keep admission constraints are applied by correctors and are themselves subject to capture (Proposition 6.7(3)); detection and the verification that capacity is kept in practice are theirs; and assessment that success has captured does not count toward coverage. A design whose correctors success can buy meets R1 on paper only, since effective liability falls to zero as rule-making is captured [thm], and loses the other rows through the interfaces that Table 7.1 marks † [thm∣prem]. R3, correction that success cannot buy, is the row on which the table stands: the closure of Definition 2.4, seen from the side of what must be done.

**Where admission does not reach.** Admission reaches only what it admits (Section 6.4), and its limits bound R8a without making it unnecessary. Systems that automate a developer's own research are used first inside it, which is why R8a is stated for internal deployment too. For an openly released class the decision to release is the admission decision for every later use, after which only a liability on use where harm can be attributed remains, the route of R1. For masked classes R8a remains necessary under the alignment-tax premise. With a fixed positive tax, a sanction bounded by exclusion and detection tending to zero cannot enforce it at arbitrarily high capability (Remark 6.8); if the tax also falls, the expected sanction must be compared with that tax (C9 and C12 together). Failure of this assurance does not establish the residual hazard floor required by R5. Under the protection standard, the recommendation is admission only up to the capability at which independent control can establish safety, with stopping the decisive class (E2) or holding development to the pace of control (E3) beyond it where broad E1 has not been achieved. S9 gives each limit its mechanism.

**Sufficient routes, and form.** Four routes are sufficient and not necessary: full inbound row sums below one (Proposition 4.10(2)); the safeguards (I1)–(I4) of Section 10.3, with the liability margin that the host sets (Theorem 10.4); not building the decisive class, holding it in common custody, and detecting and answering faster than breakout, which meet R4 (Corollary 5.7(a)); and verification whose expected sanction is at least the net gain from exit (Proposition S8.5). S9 gives the margins and what each route certifies and does not. The requirements constrain functions, not institutional form: one institution can meet them, and so can mutually recognized institutions of several jurisdictions, a federation or a polycentric complex [def]. Regulated competition meets the rows its rules carry, where those rules reach every capable actor and its correctors are separated from success [def]; what a single rule-maker cannot do is the next result.

**Proposition 9.2 (No unilateral solution) [thm].** Correction and coverage are not supplied by any one carrier, or by any one rule-maker in a contest of standing, and enacting a liability is not enough while rule-making is captured. *Let selection among carriers be cumulative (Definition 3.2), with other advantages equal and a race carrier present.*
1. *While effective liability is below the private advantage, $\tau_{\rm eff}<\nu$, a carrier's unilateral contribution to the safeguards, whether lower load, correction keyed to no one or the cost of verification, lowers its payoff relative to otherwise identical carriers and is selected against.*
2. *Let no single rule-maker reach every carrier whose combined load satisfies (reach). Then no rule-maker alone meets R1 or R2, whatever it imposes. Let the $n_s$ rule-makers be in a contest of standing in which imposing the liability on the carriers one reaches is restraint in the commons of Proposition 3.7, worth $\Upsilon_s>1$ to the field per unit of its private cost. If each weighs winning by $\lambda_s>(\Upsilon_s-n_s)/(n_s-1)$, no rule-maker imposes the liability alone in equilibrium; whether they impose it jointly is governed by Proposition 8.6.*
3. *Where captured rule-making leaves effective liability $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$, load has a negative private payoff, and selection lowers it, only once $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})>\nu$: enacting $\tau>\nu$ is not enough while $x_{\rm rule}\ge1-\nu/\tau$.*

*Proof:* S9.

**Which rows need more than one party.** R2 does by definition, and so does the shared measurement of R6. R1 does by parts (1) and (2), and part (3) ties it to R3, a second-order public good that competitors do not supply (Proposition 4.4). So do the means of R4, R5 under (shared failure), R8a under $(A_\tau)$, and R9 where the contest is decided by strength (Theorem 5.13; Proposition 6.7(3); Lemma 6.3); S9 gives the reasons row by row. Where the mechanisms are active, a minimum of cooperation is therefore necessary: binding commitments covering every capable actor, with a layer of correction that no competitor controls [thm∣prem]. This is a result conditional on its models, combined with evidence and a stated standard, not a proof from first principles. Whether the present branch is one on which the mechanisms are active is Part II's diagnosis, and how much cooperation is warranted now is the judgment of Section 12 [val].

**Coverage can unravel.** A common constraint protects only while it covers every capable actor, and coverage is itself an equilibrium that can tip. A participant that leaves becomes a carrier the constraint does not reach (R2), and against it the constraint borne by those who remain is a cost borne by some, which relative selection removes (Lemma 3.3(2)). The cost of standing outside falls as more stand outside wherever sanctions are funded by participants, shared provision is worth less with fewer inside, or rule-making is captured by those outside. Where it falls from above the cost of staying, when none stand outside, to below it, when all do, staying and leaving are both stable, separated by a tipping share, as compliance and evasion are in Lemma 3.3(3) (Remark S9.1) [thm∣prem]. One large exit can carry the outside share past it, and the exits that follow need no further shock (compare minimum participation in Barrett 2003); exempting the largest participant lowers coverage with no exit at all (EV18, read in X10) [obs]. An arrangement therefore states its participation threshold, in coverage weighted by share, not in numbers of parties, and type by type, with conditions of access that keep standing outside costlier than staying however many stand outside. A design that cannot state its threshold has not shown that it can last.

Section 10 takes the table function by function: what form each row needs, and how an order that meets the rows persists through what all share rather than through its winners.

## 10. The generative alternative

> **Claim.** The reflexive structure that lets success buy control over future selection can instead make contribution fund the conditions of further contribution [def]. The way out of an optimization trap is a change in what winning confers: a better optimization of the same objective does not correct it [thm∣prem], and the abolition of selection, competition or growth is not required [val]. Sharing and provision not won in the contest can stabilize equal standing, and success that cannot buy control over its own assessment leaves the reflexive channels no gain; sharing the divisible prize among all leaves a positive selected intensity while a positional prize remains [thm], so the design keeps that position from forming or holds it in common [rec]. Where carriers differ only in load and one of low enough load is present, four safeguards in force before capture keep the host above its floor throughout iff liability exceeds the private advantage by a margin the host sets [thm]. Where coverage is complete and correction is financed by formula, the generative order persists through what all share, not through its winners [thm∣prem], and its form follows function, not an axis of centralization [def]. Whether it attracts is a conjecture [conj].
>
> **Why.** A term common to all is invisible to relative selection, so an order whose support no carrier can claim is not selected among carriers; it persists as a common constraint does, by coverage, by sanctions that leave no type a gain from evasion, and by correctors financed by formula [thm∣prem]. Each counter-mechanism requires its own form: one rule for all where coverage is the condition, many hands where independence is, common custody where a decisive position would otherwise form [def]. Every remedy for the selection of selectors is itself a selector, so no better guardian is an answer; a structure in which errors cannot grow without limit is [val].

### 10.1 The purpose

Taken out of the contest, capability stops being spent on standing. What the race dissipates, a share of the stakes that rises with decisiveness, with the shared loss and with the narrowness of the working objective (Proposition 5.2(1)), becomes available for need: medicine and public health, education, science, competent administration, infrastructure, and the long-horizon work that fragmented incentives under-supply because its value is common (Proposition S10.1). Cooperation is therefore not only a constraint on danger; its purpose is what AI can do for people when it is not the instrument by which one actor wins the common future. The joint value is large; the race consumes it, and a change in what is selected realizes it (V1).

**Authority as a productive capacity (V4).** Authority can coordinate investment, guarantee services, support learning and act on shared purposes; the ideal the theory draws on justifies it by its responsibility for improving people's lives and the historical trajectory. It enters the design as a purpose to be sustained, not as a premise that it is realized: how institutions select and form officeholders, reward service, hear unwelcome evidence and revise harmful practices are causal variables, and a stated mission is not proof of its implementation. A larger causal radius brings larger responsibility, not larger entitlement; the author's book states the principle as "where power reaches, responsibility follows" (力之所及，责之所在) [corpus: shengsheng, 2026-07-14 第十八章]. With the norm of a shared future (Section 3.7), it is the institutional consequence of the relational self.

**Provision that does not become the system.** Out of the contest, AI can be what infrastructure is: provision of the conditions of a decent life, built to meet need rather than to gain standing. Trees are the image, in the author's words "the lungs of the ecosystem": they provide shelter and fruit, ask no return for it, and do not "become the whole ecosystem" [corpus: shengsheng]. Provision that asks no return keeps no gate, and provision that does not become the whole does not close. Part I gives such provision four properties: *distributed*, many providers below the size at which influence over correctors pays (Proposition 4.3); *good enough*, stopping growth once a need is met, which keeps its hazard modes within correction and confers no decisive advantage (Proposition 5.15); *replaceable*, so that human capacity stays above its lock-in floor (Lemma 6.3); and *for everyone*, understanding and provision alike, not for a class of guardians (Section 10.4). Contribution earns revenue, contracts and recognition; success does not buy the right to appoint evaluators, set thresholds, exclude entrants or raise their cost of entry, or amend the governing rules (V5), which cuts the conversion of success into rule support (Section 4) and keeps the incentive to contribute. What is open is who builds and funds such provision, not who holds it: provision that does not become the whole is held by no one. Section 10.5 and Discussion X10 develop one candidate answer: provision financed by formula and built by many providers under a cap on any one's share.

### 10.2 What winning confers: sharing, convertibility and provision beneath the game

The prize has a divisible part $\Omega_e$ and a positional part $\Omega_s$ (Section 3.4), and the levers act differently on the two parts and on the channels of the loop (Proposition S2.4).

The most direct change takes the frontier out of the contest by pooling effort. Where the value of capability is common to all and concave in total intensity, private incentives give too little intensity, not too much, and the joint optimum must still respect the host's floor (Proposition S10.1); where winning still confers dominance on whoever is outside the pool, what pooling changes is the set of equilibria.

**Proposition 10.1 (A common frontier) [num].** In the contest below, a frontier that pools effort and confers no dominance among those who pool it makes cooperation an equilibrium beside the race while the value of winning is moderate, and the effort each cooperator must contribute to the pool rises steeply with that value. *Of $n$ actors, cooperators hold effort $e_c$ and racers choose theirs; whoever wins the decisive contest gains $\Omega$, and every loser bears $L_b$. Each actor pays $c$ per unit of its own effort and bears $\ell$ per unit of total intensity, as in Section 3.4. Under separate frontiers each effort enters the contest separately; under a common frontier the $n_c$ cooperators pool their effort into one entity of strength $(n_ce_c)^r$, whose common value $\Omega_c$ is split equally and confers no dominance among them. Trust in a rival is the probability, independent across rivals, that it cooperates. With $n=4$, $\Omega=\Omega_c=L_b=c=1$, a shared loss $\ell=0.3$, $e_c=0.05$ and $r=1.3$: under separate frontiers the only pure equilibrium is all racing; under a common frontier all cooperating and all racing are both strict equilibria, and cooperating is a best response iff trust in each rival exceeds about 0.61; and, with $\Omega_c$ and $L_b$ fixed, the effort each cooperator contributes to the pool, $e_c$, at which racing against cooperators stops paying more than cooperating rises from 0.0054 at $\Omega=0.5$ to 0.035, 0.14 and 0.49 at $\Omega=1$, 2 and 4.*

*Scope.* One parameterization, with racers' intensities at the symmetric fixed point of best responses, each verified as a global best response; cases without a symmetric pure equilibrium are excluded. S10 gives the variations, among them a deviator that best-responds to fixed intensities, which needs more trust. *Proof:* S10 [num: legacy v4.5, N22].

A common frontier does not end the contest. Allocation within a joint project is itself a contest, and the project, as the largest carrier, has the largest return to influence over its correctors (Proposition 4.3). Effort in the pool benefits every member alike while its cost falls on the contributor, so selection within the pool favours the member that supplies least (compare Lemma 3.11), and Discussion X10 therefore sets contributions by formula. When the value of winning is large, keeping ahead of those outside the pool is the race moved up a level.

**Proposition 10.2 (What sharing, pooling and convertibility change) [thm].** Sharing and provision not won in the contest can stabilize equal standing, and cutting the conversion of success into control removes the reflexive channels; sharing the divisible prize among all leaves positive selected intensity while a positional prize remains; pooling complete payoffs and sharing only among the others have different boundary cases. *In the contest of Section 3.4, with prize $\Omega=\Omega_e+\Omega_s$:*
1. *Pooling payoffs, so that each actor receives $1-\beta$ of its own payoff and $\beta$ times the average of all, makes an actor holding $\theta_0\in(-1,n-1)$ act as if it held a self-model that rises strictly with $\beta$ and reaches $n-1$ at $\beta=1$. At $\theta_0=-1$, and under relative selection, pooling scales payoff differences by $1-\beta$: for $\beta<1$ it preserves choices and orderings, while at $\beta=1$ every relative payoff difference is zero, so selection is neutral and every choice is optimal for the relative objective.*
2. *Sharing a fraction $\beta$ of the divisible prize, with the promise kept with probability $\varrho$, is the contest with the effective prize $\Omega_{\rm eff}=(1-\beta\varrho)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$ when the shared part is divided among all $n$, the winner included, and $\Omega_{\rm eff}=\big(1-\beta\varrho\,n/(n-1)\big)\Omega_e+\Omega_s$ when it goes to the $n-1$ others, the convention of Proposition 3.5(4) and Proposition 5.8. Where $\Omega_{\rm eff}>0$, equilibrium intensity under every common self-model, dissipation and the race hazard are proportional to it, and under relative selection in one field with $r\le1$ the uninvadable intensity is $r\Omega_{\rm eff}/(nc)$. At $\Omega_{\rm eff}=0$, positive costly intensity is selected against; the positive-interior formulas do not cover $\Omega_{\rm eff}<0$. It is positive for every $\beta\varrho<1$ when the share is divided among all, and for every $\beta\varrho<(n-1)/n$ when it goes to the others; with $\Omega_s>0$ it is positive for every $\beta$ under the first convention, since then $\Omega_{\rm eff}\ge\Omega_s$, and under the second iff $\Omega_s>(\varrho n/(n-1)-1)\Omega_e$, reducing to $\Omega_s>\Omega_e/(n-1)$ when the promise is kept with certainty.*
3. *Where capacity receives provision $k_0$ not won in the contest, a smaller convertible share stabilizes equal standing (Proposition 2.3(1)); without provision the convertible share sets only the level of capacity, with or without diffusion, so winnings made non-convertible stabilize standing only together with provision. Where success cannot be converted into control over correction ($\chi=0$), the reflexive channels of Proposition 2.2 carry no gain, the return to influence over correctors is zero (Proposition 4.3), and the correction that evaluation requires falls to its value without second-order selection, $A/c_{\rm ev}$ (Proposition 4.5(1)).*

*Scope.* Parts (1) and (2) hold in the one-shot contest and its relative-selection version; part (3) collects results stated with their conditions in Sections 2.2 and 4. *Proof and numerics:* S10 [num: SH].

**Three levers, and where each acts.** Pooling widens the effective self of an actor that values its absolute payoff. Under relative selection, partial pooling slows selection without changing its direction; complete pooling makes selection neutral. It therefore does not by itself select a cooperative conduct. Sharing scales every channel of the loop by $1-\beta$ only if it is credible: contemporaneous, escrowed or under shared control, before decisiveness; promised for after a decisive win, it is worth breaking and raises the value of a decisive position instead (Proposition 5.8). Changing what winning converts into acts channel by channel (Section 2.2): through capacity only together with provision, which, growing with the stakes, holds still the won share $\omega$ on which the scissors turn (Proposition 5.17); through rule support by correction, where the cut $\chi\to0$ removes the reflexive closure outright.

**The positional part cannot be divided.** Toward it a rival's standing is one's own subordination, $\kappa_s\le0$ in (8), and relative concern is accurate (Proposition 3.6(2)). Sharing does not reach it: however much of the divisible prize is shared, the uninvadable intensity stays at least $r\Omega_s/(nc)$ when the shared part is divided among all (and, when it goes to the others, while $\beta\varrho\le(n-1)/n$), and under the second convention it stays positive for every $\beta$ iff $\Omega_s>(\varrho n/(n-1)-1)\Omega_e$ (part (2)) [thm]. Lowering decisiveness does not remove it, since a lottery can still award a position beyond sanction. It can only be kept from forming, by not building capability of the decisive class and by verified limits on what any winner may do, or held in common custody, so that no party can use it alone (Corollary 5.7(a)). That is the crux of design that Section 3.4 located: what a design must limit is what winning confers.

**What should diffuse.** Diffusion is the third inflow not tied to rank and lowers the gain in every channel (Section 2.2), so openness is a lever, within the lines drawn below. It lowers the gain without changing the game, and what diffuses is leverage as well as provision: open models are, in the author's words, "a huge leverage", "both good and bad"; open, they do not settle the problem "as long as there are still this intelligence compounding like dynamics", and with "verification … processes that you cannot fine tune those models or train those models to do other dangerous things" openness is "definitely the direction. But that direction does not mean execution" [corpus: meta-attractor, 2026-10-02, 00:26:09–00:27:19]. The line is drawn by function and by the enforcement and coverage thresholds of Table 7.1, not by who leads. Knowledge, useful services and provision diffuse. Capability of the decisive class does not, since every actor it reaches gains a deviation that can become decisive before it is answered and must satisfy (14) on its own (Proposition 5.4); nor do the weights of systems in classes that admission constrains, which no admission reaches once released; nor hazardous variety beyond contestable coverage, which raises the uncovered share that survival needs to stay summable (Theorem 5.13). What does not diffuse is held in common or not built [def].

**Remark 10.3 (Concentration at two orders).** At the first order more contenders make it likelier that one of them starts the race (Proposition 5.1); at the second, cultivated influence over correctors rises with a high moment of shares (Proposition 4.5(4)), which calls for removing common control. The design that serves both merges the frontier and disperses and insulates correction, keeping several lineages inside the merged frontier against common-mode failure (Proposition 6.9). Neither "centralize" nor "decentralize" is the answer, because the two orders call for opposite forms.

### 10.3 Safeguards, verification and participation

Four safeguards make consequences return on the host of Section 3. **(I1)** A liability above the private advantage of load, enacted in advance for anticipated harms and on the alarm for others, so that effective liability exceeds the advantage once rule-making is uncaptured; for harms beyond the assets of the liable this is regulation in advance rather than liability (Shavell 1984), and near a threshold a quantity does better than a price (Weitzman 1974). **(I2)** Independent correctors in evaluation and rule-making, with $d(t)$ above $d^\ast(t)$ by a margin. **(I3)** Independent evidence: masking kept below its threshold, or a conservative alarm on a bound on masking established independently of the field (Remark 4.9). **(I4)** All three in force before the corrector domains are captured.

**Theorem 10.4 (Sufficient safeguards, their margin, and a necessary condition) [thm].** The four safeguards return consequences to their source, and keep the host above its floor throughout iff liability exceeds the private advantage by a margin that the host sets. *Let selection be replicator dynamics among carriers that differ only in load $e\in[e_{\min},e_{\max}]$, with a carrier of load $e_{\min}$ present, on the host (3) with floor $V_{\min}$, and let $h_Ve_{\min}<\rho(1-V_{\min})$.*
1. *Sufficiency, with a margin. Under (I1)–(I4), effective liability exceeds the private advantage after a finite time, for unanticipated harms after an alarm that fires above the floor, and the load falls to $e_{\min}$; on the host (3) the long-run level $1-h_Ve_{\min}/\rho$ exceeds the floor. From a state above the floor at which a constant margin $\Delta=\tau_{\rm eff}-\nu$ takes force, the trajectory stays above the floor throughout iff $\Delta\ge\Delta_{\min}$; a margin that varies and stays at least $\Delta_{\min}$ suffices, and one that starts below it and rises can also suffice, since the whole path of the margin enters ((3)), a threshold set by the host, the floor, that state's composition and the speed of selection. Below it, the host of (3) dips under its floor and recovers. A host that regenerates less at every level and degrades by itself below a threshold $V_u$ does not recover, so there the margin must also keep the transient above $V_u$, and the floor must be set no lower than $V_u$ for crossing it to be the first loss.*
2. *Non-redundancy. For each of (I1)–(I3) there are parameters satisfying the other two under which the host crosses its floor, and parameters under which all three come into force only after capture and the host crosses it.*
3. *A necessary condition. Under (reach) and cumulative selection (Definition 3.2), with a race carrier present, a trajectory viable at every horizon keeps the cumulative advantage $B(t)=\int_0^t(\nu-\tau_{\rm eff})\,ds$ from tending to $+\infty$, and so has long-run average effective liability, the lim sup of its time average, at least the private advantage.*

*Scope.* The margin belongs to the transient, not to the long run, and on a host that degrades by itself a shortfall is permanent. Part (3) is necessary, not sufficient: a time average that tends to the advantage while the cumulative advantage grows without bound lets the host cross its floor. S10 computes the margin on both hosts and the counterexample [num: HO]. The theorem computes, for one fixed strategy, an instance of the boundary of viable parameters, not the correctable kernel of Definition 8.1, which ranges over strategies and disturbances. Enactment is not force: liability enacted while rule-making is captured acts as $\tau(1-x_{\rm rule})$ (Proposition 9.2), which is what (I4) is for. *Proof:* S10.

Verification by rivals is correction too, and Section 4 applies to it. A rival's verifier loses capture eventually under the initial-state and uniform-margin conditions of Proposition S10.2, with its full inbound row counting conversion from the verified party, from its own side and from shared sources such as models, supply chains and professional paradigms. Current independence also needs evidence on inherited rule support and captured share over the transient; rivalry keeps the verified party's contribution near zero and bounds nothing else in the row. Rivalry checks compliance, not judgment, and an accusation between rivals can trigger prevention, so joint resolution precedes any response (S10).

Participation has its own conditions (Proposition S10.3). Under complete information an arrangement is efficient for a pair of types iff its survival-weighted joint value exceeds the sum of their survival-weighted race payoffs, and an overwhelmingly strong actor takes part only for a share near its race payoff. Under private, independent types, with participation decided before they are revealed and without verification, each type's interim share must be independent of its report, and the arrangement is feasible iff the joint value covers the sum of the types' best race payoffs; it can therefore fail where strong types are rare although cooperation is efficient for every pair [num: legacy v4.5, K7], the mutual optimism that makes bargaining fail under private information (Fearon 1995). Verification that reveals types removes the information rents, and the constraint relaxes as survival in the race falls below survival under the arrangement. Participation benefits, voting rights and protection against unilateral domination are kept apart: the first is made individually rational by shares of value and access to services, the second need not follow capability, and the third is fixed first and not traded for participation. Where participation and the independence of correction cannot both be had, the design is infeasible for those actors and says so, rather than buy participation with control over correction.

### 10.4 The generative loop and its closure

The reflexive structure of Section 2 has no sign of its own: what success converts into decides whether the loop consumes the shared conditions or renews them. The author's book states the two signs as two loops of the same structure [corpus: shengsheng, 2026-07-14 第十四章]. In the *generative loop*, security makes trust possible, trust cooperation, cooperation abundance, abundance sufficiency and slack, and these allow wider care and longer foresight, which make better order and more security (安全→信任→合作→富饶→满足与余裕→更广之仁、更远之智→更好之序与安全). In the *degenerative loop*, fear produces defence, defence struggle, struggle seizure and concentration, and these scarcity and resentment; trust collapses, and fear deepens (恐惧→防御→斗争→掠夺与集中→稀缺与不满→信任之崩→更深之恐惧). The first turns more agents into generators; the second turns more of them into defenders, instruments or exiles. In institutions the generative loop runs from shared capability to distributed agency, to plural observation, criticism, maintenance and repair, to the renewal of the commons, and so to a greater capacity for cooperation and further shared capability. It is carried by funding, education, stewardship and maintenance, shared infrastructure, independent evaluation, conflict resolution, succession, protection against enclosure, real exit and appeal, and the material security that makes restraint survivable [corpus: meta-attractor]. Goodwill is not a carrier.

The criterion is generative or degenerative, not cooperative or competitive. Cooperation on the wrong thing can be worse than competition on it: a cartel is cooperation, and inclusion that stops at a club over-produces the harm it does not bear (Proposition 3.12). Bounded competition in products, science and service continues inside a generative order; what is removed is competition for control over the shared conditions and over the correctors.

Each loop of Section 7.1, and the drive behind them, has a dual: a strand of the generative loop with a carrier, a condition and a test. The five strands are the marks G6, G2, G5, G3 and G7 of Section 8.2 stated as loops; G1 and G4 take the forms of the safeguards of Section 10.3 and of custody in Section 10.5.

| Strand | Dual of | Carrier | Condition | Test |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Reasoning instead of winning** (讲理 rather than 讲赢) [corpus: alignment-selection; jian-and-wenyan] | The naturalized narrative, and the alienated language that carries it | Derivations from public premises; verifiable evidence; protocols agreed in advance; costly signals | Objectives not opposed on the topic of talk: an effective bias below $\frac14$ (Proposition 5.16) | Informativeness between rivals rises as contested stakes fall |
| **Reward separated from advantage** | Compounding | Separation of reward from authority over correction; sharing as gains arise | A structural gain below one with margin, evaluated at the present distribution of standing, not at parity, since cultivated conversion into correctors grows with a high moment of shares (Proposition 4.5(4)); $\chi\to0$; a cap on any provider's share of essential services | Conduct changes in proportion to what is shared; where it does not, advantage runs through channels outside the shared prize (C6) |
| **Provision beneath the game** | The drive: the belief in a struggle for survival, and the fear of falling behind | Shared provision, good enough, distributed and replaceable: the $k_0$ of the loop gain, acting through capacity | Not tied to standing; no inferiority in receiving it; growing with the stakes, so that the won share $\omega$ does not rise (Proposition 5.17) | The weight on relative position falls where provision is secure (C8); $1+\theta$ rises with provision relative to the stakes (Test Γ) |
| **Correction in common hands, plural and decorrelated** | Capture of the rules, and adopt or be out; masking | Certified correctors; lineages that do not fail from one cause; audited disclosure of conversion; exit from providers | Funded by formula, appointed at arm's length, growing at the rate of Proposition 4.5, raised by $w\delta_h$ where it rests on human capacity (Proposition 4.6) | Correctors' row sums stay below one as the field grows, and their misses stay uncorrelated |
| **Pace bound to understanding** | Regulator lag | Capability and effective-compute thresholds tied to measured coverage per hazard class (E3); stopping and rollback where variety already exceeds coverage (E2); drilled human capacity | $a\tau_c<1$ with margin for each hazard mode (Proposition 5.12); the uncovered share summable (Theorem 5.13) | Catch rates of planted defects at fixed human hours stay level as capability grows (C3a) |

For persons, provision beneath the game removes the ground of the belief that one must defeat others first. In the corpus's words, the common foundation does not prescribe how anyone lives; it lets everyone be entitled to live without first defeating others (共同基础不是规定所有人如何生活，而是让所有人不必通过战胜别人，才有资格活着) [corpus: shengsheng, 至善 §三]. For states, service obligations and common custody act on survival itself; for providers, $k_0$ is literally capability not won: a common layer of compute, evaluation infrastructure and services. Exit is free from providers and services, where it is selection with the opposite sign, and not from the common constraints, whose protection depends on there being no outside to them (Lemma 3.3(2)). How each strand keeps obtaining resources, meets free riding and the pressure to expand, stays correctable and avoids becoming the whole is answered function by function in Discussion X10.

**How the generative order persists.** In the meta-attractor of winning the loop runs through the winners: success buys support for the rule that selected it. In the generative order it cannot, because the second strand cuts that conversion, and support that no carrier can claim is a term common to all, which relative selection cancels (Lemma 3.3); the order is therefore not selected *among* carriers (Proposition 4.4). Carriers inside it are still selected, for usefulness; the rule is not, and it persists as a common constraint does: by coverage, so that no one can step outside the common floor; by financing fixed in formula, so that its correctors depend on no one's success; and by a loop that runs through the whole, not through its winners, in which provision, security and slack make the rule worth keeping to everyone at once, while correctors funded and appointed so that no one keys them keep it correctable. A design in which those rewarded for contribution come to hold the positions that maintain the rule takes the path of the imperial examination, created to dilute hereditary capture and turned over centuries into a selection for the examination's form: an anti-attractor can become an attractor. The corpus counts this the most serious objection to its own prescriptions, every one of which is an institutional damper [corpus: meta-attractor].

**Is the generative order an attractor?** Because it is not selected among carriers, whether it attracts is a question about the whole-level loop and about coverage, and it is a conjecture [conj]; its persistence has to be designed and measured. The same mathematics gives one caution: an order whose loop gain greatly exceeds its damping also resists the correction of its own errors, and a balancing loop that answers late overshoots (Remark S2.3). Plurality of purposes, correctors and lineages is therefore a requirement, not a decoration (Section 3.7), and it does a second job: an order whose forms can fail and be replaced can be reformed in bounded time, where one that has frozen cannot (Section 11.4).

**The reform dilemma.** Reform needs influence, and the game is what produces influence. The path by which a reformer gains the capacity to change the rules runs through the selection the reform is meant to change, so wherever that selection has closed, it removes the reform before the capacity to make it arrives: those who reach the positions from which rules are changed are those the rules favoured, and they arrive formed by them. This is the guardian problem in motion, and neither of the theory's two answers is a better guardian. Reform can act where the selection has not yet closed: at a keystone while its window is open (Section 4.5), or in a crisis with a design prepared, and its activation committed, in advance (Sections 11.4 and 12.1). And the capacity to reform can be built outside the contest for influence, by correction funded by formula and keyed to no one, so that it does not have to be won.

**Remark 10.5 (The guardian test).** Every remedy for the selection of selectors is itself a selector. Philosopher-kings, priestly classes, enlightened stewards and new universities have each been proposed as the guardians who would hold the line, and each is a corrector to which Section 4 applies; the author's question to every such proposal is how to resolve "the corruption of the constraining mechanism itself" [corpus: meta-attractor]. No better guardian is an answer. The author's book gives the mechanism and the alternative: regulation can itself become a reinforcing loop, power producing resources and resources proving the power necessary, so it cannot be entrusted to one unconstrained final controller but must divide its powers, publish its reasons, be reviewed on a schedule, receive independent feedback, and allow appeal and exit. The regulators must themselves be regulated (调节者亦须被调节), and real order is not finding people who are always right but building a structure in which error cannot grow without limit (真正之秩序，非得永远正确之人，乃立错误不能无限扩大之构) [corpus: shengsheng, 2026-07-14 第十四章]. Every arrangement and every institution within it, this section's provision, commons of understanding and correctors included, is therefore asked four questions before adoption, since each can become a position that winning reaches. *Capture:* are its correctors' full inbound row sums below one, counting every party and shared source (Proposition 4.10)? *Withdrawal:* does every closure it makes state what would end it? *Totalization:* can it make itself the whole, so that "protecting the whole" becomes a purpose no one can challenge? *Distribution of understanding:* does understanding go to everyone, or to a class whose position depends on others' not understanding? The author's answer to the last is that "everyone has access to the understanding itself directly" [corpus: alignment-selection].

### 10.5 Design by function

The design question is usually posed along ideological axes, centralization or decentralization, the individual or the collective, markets or states, and the mechanisms support neither pole. Each counter-mechanism of Part I requires its own form: coverage requires one rule for all, independence many hands, and a position beyond sanction must be held in common or not formed. In the author's words, "I don't think decentralization is the fundamental issue. It is the molochlization selection process that makes [inaudible: 'centralization' or 'decentralization'] dangerous" [corpus: meta-attractor]. The last column names the rows of Table 9.1 that each form answers, and every row has a form; Table S9.2 carries each row on to its threshold, mark and test.

| Function | What the mechanism requires | Form that follows | Answers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Common constraints: admission, the return of consequences, levies | Coverage of every capable actor; for every type that can evade, an expected sanction at least its gain from evasion; equal incidence, so that the rule does not re-sort the compliant (Lemma 3.3) | **One rule for all** | R1, R2, R8a, R8b |
| Custody of capability of the decisive class | No unilateral holding before a decisive position can form (Corollary 5.7(a)) | **Common custody, or not built** | R4 |
| Correction and evaluation | Full inbound row sums below one (Proposition 4.10); growth at the required rate (Proposition 4.5); measured correlation of misses, and control evaluations in which the red team knows the protocol (Proposition 6.9) | **Many hands**, certified, of different lineage, funded by formula | R3a, R3b |
| Who sets standards | One standard with many measurers makes standard-setting a keystone (Section 4.5), on which Goodhart acts | **Certified, rotating standard-setters, separate from measurers and measured**, with held-out metrics committed in advance | R3b, R6 |
| Measurement and language | Shared, checkable, dynamic indicators (Remark 4.9) | **One standard, many measurers** | R6 |
| Disclosure of conversion data | Every certificate rests on these data, and omission is the cheapest capture (Lemma 3.3(3)) | **Mandatory, audited disclosure** from every party, the drafting parties included, sanctioned above the gain from omission | R3a, R3b |
| Pace | The uncovered hazardous share summable; $a\tau_c<1$ with margin for each hazard mode (Theorem 5.13, Proposition 5.12) | **Pace bound to understanding:** capability thresholds tied to measured coverage per class and input thresholds that tighten with algorithmic progress (E3), with stopping and rollback where variety already exceeds coverage (E2) | R5 |
| Human decision authority | Among actors who price in a certain lock, keeping AI out of the decisions it would lock is a strict equilibrium without trust (Proposition 6.4); human capacity above its lock-in floor (Lemma 6.3) | **Narrow E1 first, broad E1 as the scope** (Section 11.3), with drilled human fallback and an evidence channel independent of AI | R9 |
| Alignment and control research | What lowers the tax is a public good that competitors undersupply; masked divergences escape evaluation (Proposition 6.7, Lemma 3.11, Remark 6.8) | **Commonly funded, openly published research**; at admission, control protocols that do not assume the system's cooperation, by capability threshold | R8a, by lowering the tax and bounding masked classes |
| Learning about the design itself | Global, irreversible failures allow no second attempt; reversible ones can be learned from (Lemma 3.11) | **Bounded parallel variants above a common floor**, with shared evaluation and adoption by evidence | — |
